The Consulate at Tampico reported that the freight
car shipped from Manzanillo to Tampico contained:
Metal

This car carrying contraband arrived in Manzanillo
on October 7, 194l, consigned to Antonio Llorens, but was
despatched on Ootober 9, 1941, to Tampico where it arrived
on October 14. The car was detained by customs authorities
and opened on October 16 under court orders. The car was
originally consigned by Carlos D. Curtis, Mexico City,
and was despatched at Mexico City by Fernando y Morales.
It is not known whether the shipment was destined for ex-
port or merely sent to Tampico for hiding. The Tampico
oustoms

=== Page 2 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
oustoms brokerage firm of González, Saldozo y Zorilla
are suspected of being interest parties and there is
reason to believe that there was corruption of railway
personnel in connection with the car which carried the
shipment to Tampico.
It appears that A. Z. Phillips, a Polish Jew, but
naturalized Mexican was the agent for the shipment, very
probably for the firm of Nagabuchi. It has been discovered
that on July 12, 1941, there was transferred from the
Bank of America, National Trust and Saving Association,
San Francisco, by Mitsuibishi, San Francisco to Mitsuibishi,
Mexico City, the sum of $325,O00.00, United States currency.
The money was sent to the Banco de Comercio in Mexico City
It has been confidentially ascertained that the money was
for the purchase of mercury. An investigation is being
made as to whether this money was used for the contraband
purchases as well as to the distribution of the money in
Mexico.
The Embassy has been informed that President Manuel
Avila Camacho has given orders that a complete investiga-
tion be made regarding the contraband shipments. While
it is realized that the President is sincere in issuing
the order, there is the possibility that the officials
carrying on the investigation will not furnish him with a
true report.

=== Page 6 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF COMMERCIAL TREATIES AND AGREEMENTS
December 6, 1941
A-A
Mr. Acheson
FW894.24/1797
The attached file appears to involve the responsi-
bilities of other Divisions rather than TA. It concerns
the Mexican Government's cooperation in preventing, at
our request, a shipment of from 10,000 to 20,000 bales
of cotton to Japan. In assuring our Embassy that he
would not permit this shipment, the Mexican Foreign
Minister stated that in view of the hardship that would
be worked upon the sellers, who were undoubtedly acting
in good faith, he believed it only just that a market
be found for the cotton in the United States. (Embassy's
telegram no. 639, November 19).
In contrast with the Mexican Government's coopera-
tion in this case, a routine report from our Embassy
in Peru (telegram no. 626, November 22) indicates that
sales of Peruvian cotton to Japan during the week ended
November 20 amounted to about 6,500 bales. Further
inquiry indicates that total shipments of cotton from
Peru to Japan from the beginning of the current crop
year,

=== Page 7 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2-
year, August 1, through November 20, have amounted to
54,776 bales. While this is not a large supply of
cotton in relation to Japan's requirements, it is larger
than Mexico's total average exports of cotton during the
past four years, and larger than Peru's exports of cotton
full
to Japan during any recent calendar year with the excep-
tion of 1940. Thus, Peru's market for cotton in Japan
has apparently been expanding at the expense of Mexico
and other countries which are cooperating in our export
control policy as regards cotton (no shipments of cotton
to Japan have been made since August from Mexico, Brazil,
Argentina, or Paraguay).
This situation raises the following questions which
you may possibly wish to bring to the attention of the
Economic Operations Board:
1. whether our hemisphere export control policy
as regards cotton is entirely consistent;
2.
if our policy is inconsistent in this case,
whether it may also be inconsistent in regard
to other commodities;
3.
whether there are any measures we can take, in
addition to our general program of economic
assistance to the other American republics,
which would help alleviate specific cases of
hardship brought about as a result of the
cooperation of those countries in our export
control policy.
As

=== Page 8 === (confidence 0.97)
3-
As indicated by these questions, the Mexican cotton
case under reference does not appear to indicate a lack
of uniformity and consistency in our cotton policy but
rather a lack of uniformity and consistency in our export
control policy as regards cotton and possibly other
commodities. For the reasons given in the attached
draft reply of November 25 to telegram no. 639 of
November 19 from our Embassy in Mexico, it is not believed
that it would be feasible to provide a market in the United
States for the cotton in question and thereby alleviate
any hardship which may result from the Mexican Govern-
ment's cooperation in preventing the export of this
cotton to Japan.
However, if it is considered desirable, you may
wish to request the appropriate Division to add to the
proposed telegram to Mexico either a reference to our
general program of economic assistance, or a reference to
possible additional measures which might be directed to
the relief of specific cases of hardship resulting from
the cooperation of other Governments in our export control
policy.
m
Harry C. Hawkins

cc to Mr. Collado

=== Page 10 === (confidence 0.99)
Mercury Due
To Go To Japs
Shows Increase
Mexicans Discover
Much Contraband
In Bronze Scrap
By Associated Press
MEXICO. CITY, Oct. 19.—C-
traband mercury recently discov-
ered in Manzanillo as it wàs
about to be loaded aboard 
Japanese steamer Azuma Mar
probably will total considerably
more than was at first estimated,
Federal Attorney General Jose
Aguilar y Maya indicated.
Of the 350 drums labeled
"Bronze Scrap," which were seiz-
ed there, 162 have been opened,
Aguilar y Maya said, and found
to contain 1,117 flasks of the
precious war metal. This is an
average of roughly seven flasks
to a drum.
When the contraband was dis-
covered two weeks ago, customs
agents in the Pacific port then
calculated the drums would only
average five flasks each.
Exportation of Mexican mer-
cury outside the Western Hemis-
phere was prohibited by a decree
of President Avila Camacho sex-
eral weeks ago when the United
States and Mexico signed an anti-
Axis economic defense pact.

Dear Reinstein,

F
As I mentioned to you this
afternoon it has been reported to us from Shanghai
from an A-l source that "North American Syndicate"
(it is not clear whether this is a name or a des-
cription) who are known to have been importers of
diesel oil for Japanese interests hope to arrange,
probably through the Cathay Oil Company, to import
a further tanker cargo of several thousand tons.
The message mentions that the
U.S. Commercial Attache has been informed.
1/2
I also enclose a memorandum
based on a telegram received from London concerning
certain shipments of petroleum products from New
Orleans on April 26th and mentioning a proposed
shipment of 15,000 barrels of Whiting oil to which
certain rather suspicious features attach.
I am also writing to Mr. Yost
about both these reporte.
Yours sincerely,
John S. Dent

=== Page 18 === (confidence 0.99)
DIVISIONO
JAN1-0 1942
THE AMERICAN REPUBLIOS
DEPARTMENT OF-8TATE
011 Shir
The British Government has received
certain information which we have been requested to bring
to your attention.
The first matter concerns the disposal
of the cargo carried by Lykes Bros. vessel HYBERT which loaded
the following parcels of Lubricating Oil at New Orleans on
April.26th 1941; viz, -
5,124 wooden barrels for Yokohama
7,548 wooden barrels for Osaka
4,680 wooden barrels for Moji
5,363 wooden barrels)
for Shanghai
3,001 cases
According to our information the vessel
proceeded to Yokohama, then to Osaka, and then Shanghai.
We have no record of her calling at Moji.
The Yokohama parcel presumably was dis-
charged at that port but there appears to be some doubt as
to the out-turn of the Osaka parcel. London tell us that the
Kobe office of Carlowitz and Company (a Statutory List firm)
informed their Shanghai Office on July 8th that the HYBERT which
should have arrived at Osaka the week before with 3,328 barrels,
arrived without this parcel although there is reason to believe
that this parcel, part of the 7,548 barrels for Osaka, was in
fact on board. From information available here we learn that
the 7,548 barrels comprised two parcels, one of 2,000 barrels
being shipped by Sterns Inc. consigned to Shippers' order and
the other of 5,548 barrels shipped by Neil C. Potter, consigned
to Koyu Hanbai Kabushiki. If part of this shipment was discharged
at some destination other than that declared on the Export
License Application, a breach of the regulations may be in-
volved. It should be noted that one of the firms involved is
Statutery,liated.
/The next

=== Page 19 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
The next point of interest is the disposal
of the Shanghai parcel. 1,325 barrels and 3,00l cases were
shipped by'the California Texas Oil Company and consigned
to the Texas Company (China) Ltd. and there appears to be
no question in connection with this parcel but London are
concerned regarding the balance of 4,038 barrels. These
were shipped by Sterns Inc. to the order of the National
Commercial Bank Ltd. for account of the Intercontinental
Trading Company, the latter Company being also on the British
Statutory list. 2,610 of the above 4,038 barrels were
imported in the name of Kwong Loh on behalf of the Inter-
continental Trading Company and of these, 1,910 barrels
are reported to contain High Grade Aviation Lubricants.
Our information is that this High Grade Aviation Lubricating
Oil was definitely destined for Italy and as the outbreak of
the German-Russian hostilities preventsits on-shipment, it is
now being offered for re-sale in Shanghai.
It would be interesting, if the exportation.
of such Lubricating Oils to Japan and Occupied China was
subject to Export License at that time, to see who were shown
as the ultimate consignees and the use to which the oil was
to be put, and an investiagtion may disclose some infringement
of the licensing regulations.
The next matter of interest is the interest
apparently displayed by the above Carlowitz in July in the
impending arrival of 15,000 drums. The contents are not men-
tioned but the inference is that it is Lubricating Oil, and
from the information available it would appear that the destina-
tion is Dairen. There was a suggestion that this material
lowld
/m22 be

=== Page 20 === (confidence 0.97)
3
woold
will be documented so as to facilitate its on-shipment
to Germany ifyr.
In this connection it is observed that
15,000 barrels of Whiting Oil ordered by the Yangtze
Motors (British Statutory List) of Shanghai from Sterns
Inc., are now in the Compress Warehouse at New Orleans.
We assume this is Whiting Oil manufactured by the Standard
Oil of Indiana, which Oil takes the brand name of "Whiting".
There may be a connection between the
15,000 drums in which Carlowitz are displaying a suspicious
interest and the 15,000 barrels of Whiting Oil in New
Orleans. In the circumstances it is suggested that ship-
ments by Sterns Inc. warrant careful investigation.
British Embassy,
Washington, D.C.
11th October, 1941

MR. ACHESON
894.24/1805
Dear Reinstein,
In my letter of October toth I men-
tioned that it had been reported to us from an A-1 source
that the North American Syndicate were endeavouring to
import Diesel Oil into Shanghai.
From one of our ugual sources I now learn
that the North American Syndicate wrote on September 9th
to Mr. Glenn A. Smith of the American Petroleum Export
Company, Los Angeles, Cal., mentioning that there was a
big demand for Diesel Oil for electric light plants and
that they could buy (if supplies could be obtained) as
594.24
much as 20,000 tons for such purposes. They added that
they were doubtful whether the business could be placed
in this country in view of official U.S. disapproval.
As a result of this they requested Mr.
Glenn A. Smith to endeavour to secure supplies in Mexico
or Venezuela, mentioning that the North American Syndicate
could probably arrange the necessary transportation if
supplies were obtained.
In view of the injection of Mexico and
Venezuela into the picture I am sending a copy of this
letter to the Rockefeller Committee for their information.
Letters are also being sent to Mr. Yost and Mr. Thornburg.
You will notice that it is now confirmed
that "North American Syndicate" is in fact a name and not
a description - a point which was not clear in the previous
report.
JAN 1 2 1942
Yours sincerely,
FILED
Mr. Jacques J. Reinstein,
Department of State,
Washington, D.C.
John S. Dent

DIVISION
Dear Mrgnginletter,
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
London have instructed us to con-
sult the United States Government about a proposal put
Division of
up by the Bangkok Branch of the East Asiatic Company,
894.24/1809
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
OCT 9 1941
the effect of which it is hoped would be to interfere
Department of State
with Japanese purchases of Tin and Wolfram ore.
Our Minister in Bangkok favours
the proposal and London agree that his conclusions are
right. They want to be assured however that the United
89424
States Government has no objection and asked us to con-
PS/PLS
sult you as soon as possible.
The East Asiatic Company point out
that all open tin ore produced in Thailand, amounting to
about 500 tons a month, and the entire production of
Wolfram, amounting to about 60 tons a month, is now being
sold to exporters in Bangkok chiefly for the Japanese
NOV 2 6 1941
market. The East Asiatic Company desires to re-enter
the metal market in cooperation with a well-known local
firm, Messrs. Yip in Tsoi. The intention would be to set
up an organization to receive ore in Malaya from the pro-
ducers and/or dealers in South Thailand on a consignment
basis for sale by private tender. Sales would only be
made to approved buyers which would at present have to
inolude local Japanese buyers, but not buyers. on behalf
of enemy.interests.
/The East

=== Page 31 === (confidence 0.98)
The East Asiatic Company point out
that as a result of restrictions their export business
has virtually come to a standstill and in order to cover
overhead expenses they are very anxious to find some
fresh activity.
Sir John Crosby favours the proposal in
that it will enable the firms: named to regain a footing
in the metal market and states that the activities of
the proposed organization will not have the effect of
increasing the diversion of ores to Bangkok. He adds
that in the event of British or American buyers wishing
to participate the existence of a friendly organization
would be an advantage.
It is also mentioned by London that
this proposal if approved will result in the establish-
ment of an organization which could later be used for
preemption if such proved desirable.
I would be grateful for the Department's
comments at your earliest convenience.
Yours sincerely,
Armany
Clasifie
A. D. Marris
Mr. T. K. Finletter,
Special Assistant to the Secretary,
Department of State,
Washington, D.C.

DEPAHTMENT OF STATE
Dear Reinstein,
We have recently received from London a
fairly detailed account of the practice adopted, in agree-
ment with the Ministry of Economic Warfare in London, by
the Government of the Netherlands East Indies with regard
to trade with Japan and the China Coast.
The export of all goods to Japan, Karafuto,
Japanese Islands and Mandated Islands, Korea, Manchuria,
Kwangtung Leased Territory, and Formosa have been subjected
to license. All Japanese balances in the N.E.I. have been
merged in one blocked account into which payments for
Japanese goods purchased will be made, and against which
will be c arged the cost of any permitted exports.
Only essential imports are allowed and in
practice this at present means only textiles. In so far
as it is necessary to export goods in order to pay for such
essential imports, export licenses will be issued to ex-
port the following goods up to the following annual maxima:
In Tons
Coffee
1,800
Soft Timber
60,000
Kapok and cotton seed
3,000
Maize
60,000
Cassava roots (Tapioca)
12,000
Rattan
600
Sugar
120,000
Pepper
60
No goods produced in the Netherlands East
Indies which may assist Japanese "war notential" may be
exported even against essential imports. A list of such
goods - termed the "nil list" - has been drawn up and com-
PS/LH
prises:
bauxite
Mr. Jacques J. Reinstein,
Department of State,

=== Page 36 === (confidence 1.00)

bauxite

scrap iron

tanning materials
hard fibres, in-

cluding sisal
molasses
and jute
tea
rice
Quinine and cinchona bark are included in the nil list until
the end of 1941 when they will be reconsidered.
With regard to oil: no licenses will be
granted to export oil to Japan so long as no exports are
made from the United States. It is not proposed however to
reject Japanese Export License Applications and the Governor
General will reply to Japanese representations on the subject
that the decision is stiil under consideration in London.
Should oil shipments from the N.E.I. recommence in the cir-
cumstances mentioned above, the Netherlands Government will
allow the issue of Export Licenses of permitted grades up to
the maximum of these grades exported in the year 1935/36 but
they will be prepared to adopt a more stringent basis if the
United States do the same.
With regard to Shanghai and other China Coast
ports, exports to Japanese consignees are in principle per-
mitted but are limited to goods falling within the permitted
list. In practice however no trade takes place with Japa-
nese consignees in China. With regard to non-Japanese firms
in China, trade is limited quantitively and Netherlands Con-
sulates are consulted as to the consignee before any
individual export is permitted.
Yours sincerely,
JOHN S. DENT

Department of State
ng I reported to London that exports of cotton

ment that it should now implement the general undertaking
which it suggested about cotton exports to Japan. Under
this undertaking shipments from India during October to
Japan should be nil, except insofar as shipments are made
under unexpired, pre-zero confirmed credits or other pre-
zero contracts where payment is made in cash.

Sir:
I have the honor to acknowledge the receipt or the
Department's airmail instruction No. 4406 /of November 12,
1941 regarding the contraband shipments to Japan.
Reference is made to the Embassy's despatoh No.
DEC 1 5 1941
FILED
14194 of November 10, 1941, /summarizing the information
received from the American Consulates at Manzanillo and
PS
Tampico concerning the contraband shipments. With re-
ferenee to the statement in ExCELSIOR as to the use of the
Island of Socorro as a contraband center, the Mexican Navy.
Department officially informed the Naval Attaché's Ofrice
that it did not believe the story. Sooorro Island is
Mexican Federal Property, and is not owned by the
Amerieam

=== Page 58 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
American William Chenoweth of Nogales, Arizona as repert-
ed by the newspaper.
With reference to the EXCEISIOR story concerning
the shipment of 150 drums, each containing 5 meroury
flasks of 75 lbs., by a Japanese vessel, this probably
referred to a shipment by A. Z. Phillips on the Japanese
S/S HEIGO MARU, which sailed from Manzanillo on Septem-
ber 18, 194l. The shipment was listed on the vessel's
manifest as 150 drums of bronze manufacture. There is
no evidence to support EXCELSIOR's story that the ship-
ment contained mercury.
It is know that the Chief of Customs has two secret
service agents stationed in Manzanillo, Colima to watoh
future shipments, but the value of these agents is
doubtful. President Avila Camacho gave strict orders
that the contraband shipments be investigated, but to
date there has been no indication that the guilty
parties have been apprehended. The trouble with the
question of contraband shipments lies with the port of-
ficials and, since these officials are underpaid, they
are prone to accept graft.
It is believed that the most effective manner of
attempting to stop contraband shipments is through acti-
vities of our own Consuls. Information received by them
should be immediately transmitted to the Chief of Cus-
toms and to the Embassy for tranamission to the Ministry of
the Navy, and the Ministry of Hacienda, with a request that
action be taken.
Respeotrully yours,
For the Chargé d'Affaires ad interim:
Raligh e. Jiben
Raleigh A. Gibson.

DEPARTMENT CF STATE
In view of the Mexiean Government's attitude
towards the United States-Japanese war, 1t would seem
894.24
894.24/1818
definitely inadvisable to take any further steps at
this time with regard to stopping contraband shipments
of mercury from Mexico to Japan. While Mexico has not
formally declared war on Japan, diplomatic relations
have been severed. Consequently it would seem to be
quite indelicate to suggest what further measures
might be taken to prevent illegal shipments to a
country virtually in the enemy category.

Mr. Miller (Treasury)
State
894.24/1825
Assistant Secretary
ACHESON
Mr. Hall called at my request. I referred to the fact that
Trom time to time Mr. Hall had made inquiries regarding the
policy of this Governnent toward the shipment of petroleum prod-
ucts from the United States to Japan and that, as he knew, the
Netherlands Government had made similar inquiries. I said that
yesterday Baron van Boetzelaer called again to ask for clarifica-
tion on this point and that I wished to inform Mr. Hall of what
I had said. I then repeated to Mr. Hall the statement which I
had made to Baron van Boetzelaer, having previously refreshed my
memory from the written memorandum. The statement was as follows:
No shipments of petroleum products have gone to Japan
from the United States since the freezing order. Although three
export licenses for comparatively small amounts were granted some
time ago, the Japanese have refused to turn in as payment Ameri-
can currency which they have in hiding in this country. All
other forms of completing the transactions have been found un-
DE
satisfactory by the Department, and it is our intention to con-
tinue to take the same attitude. In other words, through the
medium of our freezing control, exports of petroleum products
to Japan have ceased. The British authorities may expect that
through the continuance of the same control the same result will
continue. Should anything occur which might warrant a reconsid-
P
British authorities before taking action.
S
/LMA

=== Page 74 === (confidence 0.98)
-2 -
I stressed to Mr. Hall that this statement was made to
him in the greatest confidence and that it should have the
smallest possible circulation and only among highest British
authorities.
I then referred to the suggestion which he had made that
the Government of India was willing to restrict its exports of
cotton to Japan and I believed to Japanese occupied China in any
month to the amount which had been exported to those destinations
from the United States in the preceding month. I informed him
that at the present time no cotton was moving from this country
to Japan and relatively small amounts to Japanexe occupied China,
and that we would procure for him as promptly as possible after
the end of each month a statement from the Collectors of Customs
of the amounts exported.
I then referred to his letter in which he had suggested
that the British authorities were prepared to stop the exports
of iron ore from British Malaya if exports of iron ore from the
Philippines were discontinued. I stated that we were trying to
get from the Philippines a statement of the amounts of iron ore,
if any, which hd already an licensed and the amount of the
unexhausted quota. Such figures as we had led us to believe that
the quota was very nearly exhausted. If this were so, it would
not be our intention to enlarge the quota. If there were no out-
standing licenses, the policy would be to have the whole matter
referred to Washington for review, with a view toward discontinu-
ing exports. If there were any substantial number of licenses
outstanding, that situation would require further study. I told
him that I would discuss this matter further with him very shortly,
as soon as we had received the essential information from the
Philippines.
a
Dean Acheson

5th November, 1941
NO46-1941
DIVISION OF
UNICATIONS AND
Dear ReinsteiNR. ACHESON
As you are aware we have received a
considerable measure of cooperation from various American
firms with regard to shipments of commodities such as
Cotton from Latin-America to undesirable destinations
in the Far East.
Our Embassy in Buenos Aires have
lately been asked by Messrs. Bunge & Born whether
they would have any objection to the sale of some
2-300 tons of Cotton from the Argentine to Japan for
shipment on a Japanese steamer. Our Embassy mention
894.24.1828
that in the case of commodities for which they have
instructions they have taken all possible steps to
discourage sales to Japan and that many firms cooperate
with them. They have not however received any instruc-
tions with regard to Cotton.
London instruct us to tell you of
this enquiry and to ask if you have any comments. There
would not appear to be any objection to our Embassy in
Buenos Aires doing what they can to discourage ship-
ments of Cottcn as of other materials to Japan, but
it would appear that London do not want to give in-
structions without making sure that you are in agreement
with them.
Yours sincerely,
Ju
John S. Dent

1
Mr. Jacques J. Reinstein,

Department of State,

REPORTED ILLEGAL SHIPMENT OF SCRAP
IRON TO JAPAN.
The Office of the Administrator of Export Control
in its January, l94l issue of Export Control Statisties
5
reports on page 99 licensed shipments of iron and steel
scrap during that month to Japan as amounting to 331,520
pounds, valued at 2,O72. Since all outstanding licenses
authorizing the export of iron and steel scrap to Japan
were revoked, effective October 15, l940, these statistics
were questioned. Mr. John Miles, Chief of the ACC reports
section. verified these figures, adding that the shipments
cleared the customs at Port Huron, Michigan during the
week ending January 4, 194l under the authority of license
no. FA-49 1ssued to Nissho Company, Limited. This
company was informed by the Department on October 7, 194
of the revocation of license No. FA-49, and the original
of this license was returned to the Department by the
Collector of Customs at New Orleans, Louisiana. The
P
endorsoments appearing on the license indicated shipments
S
from New Orleans only.
The

=== Page 83 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
The Department in a letter dated September 4, 1941
requested information from the Collector of Customs at
Port Huron in regard to these shipments. The Collector's
reply reported no record of any such exports. When the
substance of his denial was communicated informally to
Mr. Miles, he transmitted to the Department in rebuttal,
two photostatic copies of the Collector's report from
which the ACC figures were compiled. Before I could
send for an explanation of this report to the Collector
at Port Huron, this Division's duties regarding yellow
licenses were transferred to the Economic Defense Board.
On October 2, Mr. Miles informed me by telephone
that he intended to refer the matter to Mr. Monroe
Oppenheimer, Chief of the General Counsel Unit, Economic
Defense Board.

Dear Yost,
Division of
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
We are reliably informed that Madrigal
and Company of Manila, P.I. intends shipping 2,000
NOV 1 9 1941
tons of copra cake to Japan on the KONONE MARU due
Department of Sta
to sail from Manila on November 15th.
London suggests that, in view of the
fact that Madrigal have consistently been one of the
heaviest shippers of copra cake and cocoanut oil to
Japan, it is just possible that in this case knowing
that the exports of copra and cocoanut oil have been
stopped from the Phillipines, they might be intending
to ship cake with a high oil content which would be
subject to further pressing in Japan.
Normally we know that it would be uneconomical
for the consignee in Japan to extract oil by this process.
However, we have reasnn to believe that cocoanut oil is
1
in short supply in Japan, and such tactics are therefore
not impossible. London express the hope that it may be
found possible to stop Madrigal's contemplated shipment
or alternately to get the fat analysis of the copra caker
checked. This should present no difficulties as the
894.
manufacturing mill will probably have made a routine
analysis. If any abnormal fat content is disclosed it
might be possible to treat the shipment as a shipment
of cocoanut oil.
Yours sincerely,
P
S
/MW
Mr. Charles W. Yost,
Office of Export Control,
2501 Q Street,
John S. Dent
Washington, D.C.

EXPORTS OF COPRA CAKE TO JAPANIIION OP LXPORIS
AND DEFENSE AID
The Philippines normally exports annually about
894.24/1832
115,000 to 130,000 tons of copra cake, of which about one-
third comes to the United States and two-thirds goes to
Europe. The value of this export 1s ordinarily $2,500,000
to $2,750,000. In 1940 when the European market was almost
entirely cut off, exports fell to 105,000 tons, valued at
$1,370,O00, nearly all of which came to the United States.
It is presumed that 1941 figures will show a further re-
duction and that exports from now on will be negligible,
y
since shipments to Europe are impossible and shipments to
~
the United States almost equally impossible, due to the
shipping situation. PI would prefer, therefore, the
adoption of the alternative suggestion of the British
Embassy, that copra cake to be shipped from the Philip-
pines to Japan should be analyzed and, if found to con-
tain an abnormal fat content, treated as copra or coconut
oil and, therefore, subject to the licensing requirement.
Assuming that the copra cake which Madrigal and
FILED
cake
PS
/MW

=== Page 95 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
cake, it is presumably intended for cattle feeding or for
fertilizer. If it is felt desirable to limit or prevent
shipments of copra cake to Japan for those purposes, PI
would interpose no objection. It is believed that a large
part of the copra ceke produced in the Philippines could,
if necessary, be used within the Islands, though this
would probably mean a reduced revenue to the oil mills
which would probably be reflected in somewhat reduced
prices paid for copra.
JohnDavis
PI:RIGARDS:DMS

TRFEN8 9-2=-41
Dear Tom:
Enclosed is a memorandum from Mr. McElroy with accompanying
894.24/1833
statistical tables, establishing the possibility that Manila
fiber of high grades has been approved for shipment from the
Philippine Islands to Japan as a result of down-grading fibers
by the Japanese shippers.
This possibility has been further confirmed by Colonel
W. E. Chickering, Deputy Administrator of Export Control, through
Mr. Edward Browning, Jr., Liason Officer, Export Control.
Colonel Chickering has just returned from the Philippine Islands
and admits the existence of the leakage to Japan. However, he
is of the opinion that it will be almost impossible to stop the
leak unless the United States will be willing to take some of
the poorer grades of Manila fiber along with the good grades.
He reports that the Philippine dealers tend to "sweeten" the
Japanese purchases with better grades in order to make the sales.
If we took more Manila fiber, they would have less need to deal
with Japan.
There is some question as to whether this whole problem is
one for preclusive buying or whether it can best be met by a
strengthening of export control.
FILED
Sincerely yours,
1381
George C. McGhee
Stockpiling and Shipping
Imports Branch
P
S
enclosure

MrA Finlettera
PI does not see anything in the tables enclosed with
the memorandum to Mr. Elliott to indicate an alarming
situation with regard to recent Philippine abaca exports
to Japan. Shipments for the week ending August 29 were
somewhat above normal, following very small shipments in
the preceding two weeks. For the month of August they
seemed to have been only a little above normal. It is
expected that shipments will be small in September and
probably during the rest of the year, since it is under-
stood that no Japanese ships have called at the Philip-
pines since the Frozen Fund Legislation was applied to
Japan, even though most transactions between Japan and
894
the Philippines are covered by general licenses.
The High Commissioner is authorized, under present
directives, to issue licenses covering shipments of Yorw
grades of abaca to Japan to the amount of 16,000,000 pounds
or about 58,000 bales between May 28 and December 31, 1941.
This is at the rate of about 100,000 bales a year, nd 1t
contrary, the directive for 1942 will authorize 10000
6
bales

=== Page 103 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
bales. Normally, Japan has taken in the neighborhood of
130,000 bales a year of the Y0TW grades, 60,000 bales of
the grades from J2 through M (regarded as rope grades in
Europe and Japan but not much used in the United States)
and 100,000 bales of the better grades, such as are used
by rope manufacturers in the United States. The total of
290,000 bales would approximately represent average annual
shipments of abaca from the Philippines to Japan. It is
believed that Japan also receives a moderate amount of
abaca from Borneo, but no information is available as to
the amount. According to a report from the American Con-
sul in Manila, dated June 27, 1941, the minimum domestic
requirements of Japan are probably at least 200,000 bales
a year, this estimate making no allowance for abnormal
military use of the product. The Consul, who served many
years in Japan before going to the Philippines, further
states that abaca is absolutely essential to Japanese
internal economy since the bulk of consumption is in the
Japanese fishing industry which supplies one of the most
important elements in the Japanese diet. It is probable
that such rope as can be made from the Yorw grades 1s not
strong enough for heavy-duty use in the Navy, and in view
of the importance of the fishing industry it is likely that
most of it is allocated to that industry.
With

=== Page 104 === (confidence 0.95)
-3
With regard to exports to Japan, the Consul expresses
the opinion that there should be a published quota which
would be limited to a monthly or quarterly maximum, thus
permitting the industry to operate on a normal basis with
a minimum of uncertainty. At present it is understood
that the quota is secret and at the end of June Japanese
buyers were acting on the assumption that they could buy
and ship the waste grades without limit. It was antici-
pated, that at the rate of purchase then current, the
quota for the year would become exhausted within two
months.
With regard to the possibility that fiber of high
grades may have been approved for shipment to Japan as a
result of down-grading, it is suggested that the High
Commissioner be asked to look into it. All fiber exported
from the Philippines should be graded under the super-
vision of the Fiber Inspection Board. The purpose of
butice
that board has normally been to prevent up-grading, but
424
nanila
it should also be possible for it to assure that there is
of Sept.26
no flagrant down-grading of abaca exported to Japan.
ace maulo
Summing up, PI does not believe that there is any
427 of0qef25
necessity for a change in the present directive covering
exports of abaca to Japan unless there is a desire to
impose

=== Page 105 === (confidence 0.97)
-4
impose pressure on the Japanese Government. It would be
desirable, however, to obtain the opinion of the High
Commissioner, if any action is contemplated.

British Embassy

By Safe Hand
1st November, 1941
Dear Yost,
We have just received information from London that
so-called "waste" hemp from Manila is of considerable
value to the Japanese. They apparently pick it over; the
sound hemp is used for nope, and the weak and damaged for
paper manufacture. London expresses a hope that it will
be found possible to prohibit all further exports of
"waste" hemp from the Philippines to Japan, particularly
as similar Empire goods such as coir from India are now
stopped.
I would be grateful if you could let me know how tha
quota of 16,000,000 1bs. now stands. My latest information
Is that the S.S. MATAI MARU from Manila to Kobe on the 19th
September carried 25,000 bales, which were presumably quota
hemp. Are shipments still being made, or is the quota by
now perhaps exhausted?
I would also be grateful if you could let me know
whether you have any information to confirm the London
report, and if so whether you think any action is required.
Yours sincerely,
JOHN S. DENT
of State

=== Page 111 === (confidence 0.96)
ABVISERO
DE
NOV 2 4 1941
NOV211441
DIVISION
Memorandum of Dutoh Export Control Policy
by Minister van Mook Whioh Was Also
Approved By The Governor General Of The
Dutch East Indies
1. With regard to exports to Japan the policy of the
894.24/183
Notherlands Indies has twice undergone an important change.
2. Before the war the general complaint from our sid was
894.24
that the balance of trade with Japan tended to be more ad-
verse every year. The exports, however, which we would
like to see increased happened to be those of non-strategic
3
5
agricultural and forestry products like sugar, copra, coffee
and timber.
3. After May 10, 1940, the interest of Japan for our export
products showed a sudden development in the direction of raw
materials for industrial and military purposes like mineral
oil, rubber, tin and various ores. After the conolusion of
the Tripartite Pact in September, 1940, the Japanese made
additional demands for vegetable fats, fibres, tanning mate-
rials, etc., goods which were notoriously lacking in Germany.
4. The restriotive measures taken during that period by
the Netherlands Indies Government were based on three princi-
ples:
a. No exports of goods needed for ourselves or for
our allies;
b. No disorimination in favor of Japan and against
other neutral countries;
NOV 24 1941
e. No exports which would provide Japan with more tha
FILRD
its normal requirements and thus enable it to
furnish the enemy with those materials.

=== Page 112 === (confidence 0.99)
Although there was no embargo directed against Japan,
trietions mentioned had to be striotly executed and
the quantities to be exported were generally eal-
 on a basis of the known consumption in Japan in
former years; any recent inorease of consumption could not,
asrule, be taken into consideration.
A complete embargo was not deemed advisable at that
time; moreover it would not have been supported by a similar
policy of the British Empire and the United States.
7. When Japan moved South into Frenoh Indo-China, this
move was considered a direct threat against the Netherlands
Indies. Even if the United States and the British Empire
had not promptly frozen Japanese assets, we would have had
to reconsider our position. As it was we immediately joined
in this action, even though a clear system of cooperation
had not yet been established.
8. The restriotion of exports to Japan should be coordinated
in such a way that
a. Japan will not be able to get from one country
what is denied elsewhere;
the?
b. The cagbargo will effectively lower that war
potential of Japan.
9. The machinery for coordination is still rather rudi-
mentary, especially as regards the contact and the exohange
of information between the United States and the Netherlands
Indies. The alliance between the Netherlands and the British
Empire has, of course, gradually developed a closer cooperation.
It would be advisable to develop a similar system of copper-
ation with regard to export controls both in Washington
(with the Netherlands Legation) and in Batavia (with the
Amerioan Consulate General).
The embargo itself should be specially direeted

=== Page 113 === (confidence 0.98)
tiom or exporte of goods whieh
for military purpowes. It should net be
sd to starve or cripple the Japanese people, as
not required to realize the aim of lowering the
fighting strength and might enable the military
to propogate the inevitability amongst the Japanese
People. As regards war material, however, the embargo should
be as complete as possible.
11. It must not be overlooked that Japan has many, though
partly inadequate, sources of war materials within the
Japanese Empire, Manchuquo, China, Indo-China, Thailand and
possibly South America. Half way measures from our side
would not exert enough pressure, as Japan might then be
able, partly through severe restriotions on oivil use, to
maintain and even inorease its war potential, while entrench-
ing itself at leisure in Indo-China and possibly Thailand
and waiting for a better opportunity to strike.
12. Of course a tight embargo on war materials may lead
to war. Any less stringent system, however, will seem to
strengthen the position of the military party, whereas the
fear of a general war is probably strong enough at present
to deter the Japanese Government from extreme measures as
long as there is no change for the worse in the allied
situation in Europe.
15. Finally measures of an ambiguous or intricate oharacter
should be avoided, as the fear of unknown consequenees
casily leads the Japanese to react by force. A gradual
tightening of the chain may have a similar effeot; our
experaence teaches us that a olear and convincing action
1s most effeetive in the way of making the Japanese pause
donsider.

=== Page 114 === (confidence 0.98)
. Im practice this polioy should mean for the
Nothorlands Indies:
No repeat no exports of mineral oil, rubber, tin,
iron and iron ore, bauxite, nickel ore, manganese,
 resin, tanning materials, palm oil, etc.;
Small exports of fibres, copra, kapok, eto;
Normal exports of sugar coffee, maize, timber, etc.

Some time ago, shortly before the outbreak of war
between the United States and Japan, the Manager of the
Banco Minero de Bolivia, Mr. Santiago Schulze, assured
me that the last shipment of Bolivian tungsten to Japan,
exempted from the provisions of the tungsten contraot
between the Metals Reserve Company and Bolivian producers,
had left Bolivia several months ago. He also asserted
that the last shipment of antimony from Bolivia to Japan
had been made in August 1941, although there were small
amounts remaining due on contracts of the Banco Minero
with Japanese buyers. After the outbreak of the war,
Mr. Sohulze informed me that the small balanoes of enti-
mony intended for Japan had not been shipped.
In view of the fact that it some times takes over a
month for Bolivian minerals to reach Chilean ports from
mines in Bolivia and also of the faot that they are usually
still further delayed at the ports awaiting shipment, the
Legation sees no reason to doubt Mr. Sohulze's statements
dosori bed above. It seems probable that the antimony and
tungsten shown on the table aotually left Bolivia in August
or earlier.

1942 mo chrd
You will remember in my letter of
October 24th (W.T. 828/245) I told you we had a report
from an A-1 source that the North American Syndicate
of Shanghai were endeavouring to import diesel oil and
had actually reqrested Mr. Glen A. Smith of the
American Petroleum Export Company, Los Angeles, to try
and secure supplies in Venezuela or Mexico. The North
American Syndicate stated that they could probably secure
the necessary transportation if supplies were obtained.
6
From similar sources, it now appears
that the business is nearing completion. On October
894.24
10th Mitsubishi San Francisco cabled to Mitsubishi
Tokyo that an enquiry had been received in Manzanillo
from Shanghai for 10,000 tons of diesel oil, shipment
twenty days after contract, charterer Mexican Government,
receiver North American Syndicate. They also stated
that this was to be a transshipment cargo covered by
a Tampico-Shanghai through Bill of Lading issued by the
Mexican Government.
It is also known that on October 3rd
Petleonsa, Tokyo, cabled Petleonsa, Mexico City, asking
about "the permit for diesel oil". Petleonsa, you may
know, is Petroleos Leon S.A. run by Abraham Z. Phillips
for whose arrest on a charge of smuggling a warrant has
recently been issued in Mexico (according to the newspapers).
Both the firm and the owmer are on Supplement No. 3 of
the V.S. Proclaimed List.
894.241805
We also
Division of
Mr. Jaogues J. Reinstein,
FAR EASTEAN AFFAIRS
Départment of State,
FEB5-1942
Washington, D.C.
Department of State

=== Page 133 === (confidence 0.96)
-2-
We also now hear from the Government of
Hong Kong further details concerning the North American
Syndicate. This company was founded by a Mr. R. S. Boyd,
former manager of the Far East Syndicate, who took over
the business of the American Exporters, Shanghai, on September
20th. Hong Kong go on to say that they also learn from
reliable sources that the Shanghai branch of the North
American Syndicate is attempting to import diesel oil into
Shanghai, (which ties in with what we now know); and
further that money is being remitted to the American
Petroleum Export Company in Los Angeles through black
market channels. I think you will find that the files
on this company contain some interesting information.
Mr. Boyd has apparently already begun arrangements through
the Bank of Canton in Shanghai and the Banco Nacional de
Mexico, Mexico City.
I hope that this further information will
facilitate whatever action it has been found possible to
take. It would seem that the transaction as apparently
contemplated is quite likely to be effected unless urgent
steps can be taken either in Mexico or possibly in California
to prevent it.
As they received copies of my previous
letter, I am sending copies of this also to Mr. Thornburg,
Mr. Yost and the Rockefeller Committee.
Yours sincerely,
Whutdend
John S. Dent

DD
Washington, D
Sir:
22, 194l, in regard to the plan to ship Brazil's
strategic materials,to Japan via Chile, I have the
honor to report that Japanese firms in Brazil have
been

=== Page 140 === (confidence 0.96)
- 2 -
been unloading on the market stocks which they
have held of castor beans, monazite sand, mica
and quartz crystal. It appears unlikely that
these commodities would be released if the
Japanese firms owning them had any expectation
of being able to effcct their delivery to Japan
in the near future. The liquidation of these
stocks has been accelerated by the freezing
controls of the Bank of Brazil which have im-
mobilized the cash resources of Japanese firms
in Brazil.
The Embassy has verified three cases in
which Japanese companies demanded cash for their
strategic materials in an effort to obtain liquid
assets not subject to the control of the Bank of
Brazil.
Respectfully yours,
For the Ambassador:
John F. Simmons,
Counselor of Embassy.
CC: Embassy of the United States of America,
Santiago, Chile.

=== Page 146 === (confidence 1.00)
February 14, 1942
It has been reported that the expansion of important
steel mills in Japan ceased during 1939 when additional machinery
could not be.obtained from Germany, Great Britain and to some ex-
tent from the United States. For the same year the output of all
classes of steel was said to have reached 7,000,000 kilo tons.
With the extension of existing plants plainly out of the
question in 1940, increased production could only be secured by the
continuous operation of the mills. Only the milling machinery made
in Japan made it possible to keep these plants operating on a twenty-
four hour basis since July 1940.
In 1939, imports of scrap iron from all countries reached
3,000,000 kilo tons. About 4,500,000 kilo tons of iron ore were im-
ported, while the domestic production of iron ore during the same year
amounted to 400,000 kilo tons.
Imports of scrap iron from the United States, Canada and
all British possessions ceased in l94l, but imports of iron ore from
Trengganu, Johore, Manchuria, China and from newly developed sources
in French Indo China were about 5,000, O00 kilo tons. At the end of
1941 scrap iron supplies were not greater than 5,000,000 kilo tons
divided as follows:
Nippon Seitetsu at Yawata
2,000,000 ki1o tons
Kobe Seikosho
500,000 kilo tons

750,000 kilo tons

1,000,000 to 1,250,000 ki10 tons

March 10th., 1942.
Hon. Joseph H. Ball, U. S. Senator,
Washington, D. C.
Dear Senator:-In the Congressional RECORD dated March 7th.
there are references by Congressman Elliott in re a Report
of the Dies Committee, a volume of near 2,O00 pages, matters
in question the completeness (in content) of which I desire
to read: Can you obtain a copy of this lengthy Report for
the undersigned? If so, please do so.
Having read a statement or two in connection with cer-
tain Japanese matters I wish to learn about WHEN our Govern-
ment issued Orders given Commercial interests NOT to deliver
Scrap Iron and like elements (which had been going to Japan)
to Japan. It had been discussed in a Local Club here that
of Scrap Iron leaving this country about (nearly) 58% of such
material reaching Japan was shipped from the United States.
This just before the Order to cease delivering to Japan such
elements was made known. Can you turn any light" on this
matter?
Thanking you for attention to these matters,
Very respectfully,
(ReV.) CORNELIUS E. WALKER

Division of
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
MAY 5- 1942
DIVISION OF CONTROLS
Department of State
MEMORANDUM
REOEIVED
1942
April 9, 1941
DIVISION OF
THE EXPORT OF PETROLEUM PRODUCTS TO JAPAN
Gasoline
Since the imposition on July 26, 1940 of restriotions
on the export of certain petroleum products, the Depart-
ment has licensed about 9,200,000 barrels of gasoline
for export to Japan and has applications for 2 million
additional barrels now pending. The amount already
licensed represents more than twice the normal annual
894.24/1853
MAY
pre-war import of Japan from all sources and is nearly
FILHID
894.
three times the quantity of gasoline which the United 
States exported to Japan in 1940, a most abnormal year
Of this 9,200,000 barrels, approximately 4 million
have already been exported and 5 million remain to be
exported. The Department will, moreover, if new restrie-
tions are not shortly imposed, be obliged to grant the
pending applications for 2 million additional barrels.
When these licenses are issued, Japan will be in a
position to obtain in the United States this year 8 million
P
barrels of gasolime (1 million already shipped plus 7 million
S
authorized) in addition to about 4 million barrels which
/RV
she will obtain from the Netherlands Indies under existing
contracts,

=== Page 162 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
contrasts, to say nothing of what might be licensed in
this country during the remainder of the year. Her
total import from these two sources would, therefore, be
at least three times her normal pre-war import and more
than twice her extraordinary 1940 import. Such a supply
would obviously make her completely self-sufficient in
gasoline for an indefinite period of time.
The public is to some degree under the impression,
based on the President's statement of July 26 that no
exports of aviation gasoline outside the Western Hemisphere
would be permitted, that gasoline of aviation quality
can not go to Japan. This impression is wholly erroneous.
Only the very high grade aviation gasoline used by our
Army and Navy is under the export ban, and most of the
9,200,000 barrels referred to above 1s of satisfactory
aviation quality or can be made so by a very simple
process. In fact a large proportion of the license
applications state quite frankly that the gasoline is
intended for aviation use by the Japanese Army or Navy.
Lubricating O1l
The lubricating oils subject to license are only
the high grades used by our Army and Navy air forces.
The Japanese have been and are obtaining here, without
having to obtain licenses, large supplies of lubricating
o11

=== Page 163 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
oil which is either of satisfactory aviation quality
or can be made so by a simple process. Exports to Japan
1n 1940 were more than twice usual pre-war quantities.
Furthermore, reliable reports make it appear practically
certain that substantial quantities of this oil are now
being transshipped by the Japanese across Siberia to
Germany.
Other Petroleum Products
Exports to Japan in 1940 of other petroleum products
(except petroleum greases) have not been substantially
in excess of usual pre-war quantities. New trends in
this field, however, arise very suddenly. For example,
the British have recently been most alarmed over exports
of diesel oil to both Japan and Thailand, in the former
case because they fear the oil may be transshipped to
Germany for submarine use, in the latter case because
they fear it may be used by the Japanese in an attack
on Singapore or by German raiders in the Pacific.
Crude oil exports to Japan in 1940 were actually
less than in recent years, but on the other hand the
Department has since July 26 licensed approximately
22 million barrels of crude for export to Japan. This
quantity, of which some three-fourths remains unshipped,
is greater than the amount Japan has obtained from the
United

=== Page 164 === (confidence 0.98)
-4
United States in any year of the Sino-Japanese War.
It should be noted, moreover, that Japan can and is
concentrating her purchasing on those high grade orudes
from whioh high percentages of aviation gas can be
easily obtained.
Recommendations
In the belief that in these circumstanoes prompt
action is necessary, the following recommendations are
made. The proposed time table is based on the desire
of PA/H and FE to delay most of the suggested steps
until Mr. Matsuoka has returned to Tokyo and until the
Batavia contract has been renewed.
1. Request General Maxwell at once to furnish the
Department with a new and more comprehensive definition
of the aviation gasoline the export of which outside
the Western Hemisphere and the British Empire* shall be
prohibited. (This definition shall inolude all gasolines
susceptible of aviation use and shall exclude ordinary
motor gasoline. The dividing line shall presumably
be about 65 octane number without lead and 80 when
lead has been added.) On the receipt of this definition
inform
*NOTE: Where reference is made herein to restrio-
tions on exports outside the Western Hemisphere and the
British Empire, it is understood that exceptions shall
be made for countries resisting aggression.

=== Page 165 === (confidence 0.98)
-5-
inform all holders of valid licenses for exports outside
the Western Hemisphere and the British Empire to turn
in these licenses and submit new applications stating
whether or not the gasoline which it is proposed to
export falls under the new definition of aviation gas.
If it does, no new license will be issued. No announce-
ment of policy will, however, be made at this time, and
no action on the new applications will be taken until
the further steps outlined below are announced.
2. At a convenient moment after the receipt of
these new applications, presumably at the end of April,
announcement will be made that gasoline not falling within
the new definition of aviation gas will be licensed in
usual or pre-war quantities. It shall be made clear
that during the remaining two-thirds of 1941 shipment
of two-thirds of the pre-war export of motor gas will
be authorized. The base period will be the average of
the years 1935-39 inclusive.
3. General Maxwell shall be requested at once
to redefine on April 15 the aviation lubrioating oil
which 1s subject to license. This redefinition shall
extend the scope of the restriction by dropping the
viscosity index of the restricted oil from 85 to 60 or
65. Any outstanding licenses authorizing the export of
such oils outside the Western Hemisphere and the British
Empire

=== Page 166 === (confidence 0.98)
-6-
Empire shall be revoked and the holders asked to submit
new applications. At the end of April announcement will
be made that oils falling outside the new definition
will be licensed for export outside the Western Hemisphere
and the British Empire in usual or pre-war amounts. The
same procedure will be followed in granting applications
as in the case of gasoline.
4. General Maxwell shall be requested to place
all petroleum products under export licensing control
and announcement of this step shall be made at the end
of April.
5. When diesel oil is subjected to the licensing
requirement, its export outside the Western Hemisphere
and the British Empire shall be limited to usual pre-
war quantities. The same procedure will be followed
in granting applications as in the case of gasoline.
6. The export of other petroleum products shall
be licensed freely for the moment, but the question of
limiting exports of petroleum greases and high grade
crudes should be given immediate study.
7. This whole program, as soon as it has been
approved, shall be discussed with the British.
LM
Charles W, Yest

EA, FE, TA, and PA/H)

The attached despatch no. 113, dated
June 9, 1942 from the American Consulate,
Basel, Switzerland, encloses a translation
of an artiele taken from the Arbeiter Zeitung
commenting on remarks by military specia-
lists on Japan's war economy made in the
Pester Lloyd of Budapest.
In discussing Japan's economic gains
from conquered territory the author mini-
mizes Japan's returns from China and empha-
sizos the difficulties Japan faces from
continued popular oppostion. "Fair success"
is said to have been achieved by Japan in
Manchuria where "national feelings" are be-
lieved. to be less than in Korea. Manchuria,
in cooperation with Japan, is characterized
as "opportunistic". Indochina, Thailand,
and Burma are said to have aided Japan more
concretely, but the author at the same time
questions the basic friendliness of these
countries for Japan.
It is stated that Singapore is slated
to become the "economic center" for the areas
immediately north of Singapore. The author
expresses pessimism with regard to Japan's
shipbuilding plans for Singapore where all
dockyards are said to have been turned over
to

=== Page 181 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
to the Mitsubishi-Heavy Industry Concern.
While admitting that Japan made remarkable
progress in shipbuilding between 1933 and
1937 the author asserts that thə improvement
in tonnage was "not in proportion to modern
Japanese war needs". The author asserts
that Japan's raw steel production, although
6.4 million tons in 1939, is insufficient
for Japan's war needs. He makes the some-
what astonishing statement that "no doubt,
the slightest discontinuance, a single
failure in her armament production should
fathin
suffice to ruin Japan".
a.t.
While admitting Japan's "incomparable
the author suggests that Japan may place its
flreliancein such assets, and asserts that
Japan aims at establishing its own economic
sphere by means of the "cold calculating
methods of a capitalistic-imperialistic power."
The article does not appear to be a
careful study of Japan's strength and weak-
ness but is interesting as indicative of
views expressed in another Axis country.

=== Page 183 === (confidence 0.99)
no.1 to despatoh no.ll3 of Walter H. Sholes,
Consul General, Basel, Switzerland, dated June 9,
pon the subjeot of "Slightest Discontinuance in
Armament Production Should Ruin that Country"
SOURCE: ARBRITER ZEITUNG,
Basel, June 5, 1942.
(TRANSLATION)
EXPLOITATION OF CHINA
Recently, in the PESTER LLOYD, a military specialist
made some interesting comments on Japan's war economy.
This was predicated by him on the existence of three
decisive economic warfare factors: The first one being
the extent of one's own economic war needs; the second,
the breakdown of the economic conception of the enemy;
the third, the improvement of one's own war economy through
conquering valuable economic enemy territory. As regards
this last point, Japan has increased her armament potential
through ocoupying the three northern provinces of China,
as well as all the more important coastal towns and river
banks far up into the interior of China. It is said that
since the settlement of the Wang-Tsching-Wai Government
in Nanking, the mineral and agrarian raw material exploi-
tation has met with fair success in those parts. We are
under the impression, however, that, except for the North
China iron ore deposits, these Chinese conquests have but
a problematioal value; for conditions in China are still
very far from normal, as proved by recent news of partisan
battles in districts conquered hong ago. No importance
as regards the technicalities of production is attached
to the fact that Japan has incorporated the Nanking-Dollar
in the Yen currency blook on April 1, 1942. Japan has also
made endeavors to inoorporate Hongkong in the Chinese
economic sphere and thus 23 Chinese banks resumed activity
at the beginning of February; but for the present time
there is no question of Hongkong assuming its proper trade
functions, as they depend wholly upon British activities
and cosmopolitical contacts. Furthermore, it must be t aken
into consideration that the various parts of China, even
those subject to Japanese administration, all lead today
a separate life and that it has not even been possible
up to now to establish proper traffic connections between
Central and North China. The fact that an extensive ferry
service between Nanking and Pukow was established by the
Japanese last March and that the railway distance between
Shanghai and Peking will thus be reduoed by 23 percent,
is merely a matter of organization which does not sffect
the economic situation by any means.
It

=== Page 184 === (confidence 0.99)
22
It will soon be recognized, at any rate, that the
Japanese are more successful at organizing than in real
economic upbuilding. It is not intended thereby to dis-
oredit the merits of Japan itself; the industrial value
of produotion, amounting to 5.75 billion Yen in 1930,
had increased to 24 billion Yen already in 1939 : a very
big improvement even though the value of the Yen has
fallon. But in conquered districts a decisive factor
stands in their war, i.e. the population's adversion
for the Japanese. They can only be economically success-
ful where the population either is not yet susceptible to
national feelings or has an actual sympathy for Japan.
The latter has proved to be the case in Indo China; tho
first, to a certain degree, applies to the Dutch East
Indies. In China, however, the independence movement
has progressed so far as to resist the purpose of puppet
governments. And thus the ideal of a great coordinated
Eastern Asiatic economic sphere suffers right in its
roots.
COOPERATION WITH MANCHUKUO
One part of China, of course, does not enter this
category: In Manchukuo where Japan has been settled for
years, fair sucoess has been had. The coal and iron
ore mines and the soya production are being completely
exploited for Japan's benefit. It is hardly possible
to ascertain to what extent Manchukuo is subject to
revolutionary underground work - a great part of the
population consists of Chinese immigrants - but its
Government maintains strong cooperative links with
Japan and it looks as though it were able to master the
situation. Vice Minister Aoki of the Manchukuo Finance
Department, has recently discussed with Japan the question
of activating the country's production and maintaining the
coordination of industrial development in Japan and Man-
chukuo. This obodience on the part of Manchuria is
probably due to its having been badly neglected by China
while a tributary, and to its present satisfaction at
reoeiving special care and not being subject any longer
to permanent vicissitudes of Chinese life. Its neighbor,
Korea, is a strange phenomenon; though it has been in-
corporated for almost forty years in the Japanese Empire,
it still entertains strong national feelings, while Man-
churia appears to have much less of a national character.
THE HELP OF INDO CHINA
It appears evident, however, that Manchukuo does not
entertain feelings of extreme sympathy towards Japan and
that cooperation is carried on under an opportunistio point
of view. On the other hand, Indo China, Thailand and Burma
have,

=== Page 185 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
have, no doubt, given proof of their friendship for Japan-
Burma to such an extent as to greatly handicap British
warfare. Here, likewise, it remains to speculate as to
whioh classes of the population are concerned. Further-
mowe, it has long been known to us that there is a pro-
Japanese party in Thailand which cannot forgive England
and France for having continuously taken parts of their
land - one in the West, the other in the East; but we
also know that Siam patriots fear the help of Japan as
they think it would mean choosing the worse of two evils.
A great deal depends upon the feelings of the population,
for the policy of the scorched earth may only have success
when the regular war is supported by guerilla war. At any
rate, a great improvement in Japan's food question was
obtained through the Indo-Chinese rice countries. It
is reported that Indo-China's rice exports during Jan-
uary and February 1942, compared with the same months in
194i, increased by 20 percent, and that almost the whole
quantity of rice went to Japan. Singapore is now supposed
to become the economic center for all this section and it
has been given the new name Schonanto.
THE TONNAGE PROBLEM
Schonanto is likewise soheduled to become the center
of a huge shipyard industry. The leading Japanese shipbuil-
dors, the Mitsubishi-Heavy Industry Concern, according to a
state order, have already taken possession of all the dock-
jards there and are entrusted with the construction of
coastal and fishing boats and ships' machinery. It is doubt-
ful, however, whether it will be possible in so short a
time to make up for Japan's missing tonnage. In this
connection, we are only speaking of freighters and troop
ships; the substitution and construction of real war ships
belong to another ohapter. If Roosevelt has pronounced
pessimistio words concerning the tonnage question of the
United States in his recent Maritime Day speech (perhaps
he was a trifle too pessimistic on purpose!), there must
besreason for Japan to have even more pessimistic prospeots.
No figures regarding Japanese shipbuilding have been pub-
lished sinoe 1937; it is only known that in 1933 Japanese
dockyards launched not more than 170,000 gross registered
tons; in 1937 the figures had increased to 450,000 gross
registered tons, which represented a huge improvement, but
not in proportion to modern Japanese war needs. As a result
of accelerated building activities during the past few years,
Japan has the advantage of disposing of very modern ships;
while in the fall of 1939, United States merchant ships had
an average age of 20 years and English ships an average age
of 15 years, there was almost as much as one-third of the
Japanese tonnage that was less than five years old. This
faot, however, may only guarantee their longer existence
in case of normal wear; if they are sunk it works out to a
disadvantage as these new ships are not yet amortized.
Considering all faotors, it is absolutely correot, there-
fore, if, besides the questions of securing the necessary
raw

=== Page 186 === (confidence 0.99)
-4
raw materials, and of concentrating industry on war pro-
duetion, the solution of the tonnage question is indicated
as boing the third of Japan's prinoipal problems. When,
at the end of April, the Japanese Gabinet approved a gen-
eral plan mobilising raw materials for the year l942,
comprising all Japanese occupied territory, the question
of building merchant ships and of shipbuilding materials
was thoroughly disoussed.
ECONOMY AND DESTINY
In spite of complete concentration of heavy industry
production on war purposes there will be cause for many
a headache. The Japanese raw steel production has in-
creased from 283,000 tons in the year 1914 to 547,000
tons in 1939 and to 6.4 million tons in 1939, thus taking
seventh place in world production. Still, this is but one
place below Belgian production. Considering the huge soale
Japanese warfare has already assumed and will no doubt
maintain, it would not suffice even if a few million tons
were added to the aforementioned figures. Japan has of-
fered a few surprises on the outbreak of the Pacific war:
her air force was much better and larger than was expected
and she disposed of air torpedoes and tow-men submarines
which her Anglo-Saxon enemy did not possess. But it is
now that they will have to give evidence as to whether
they can keep their end up or not. Do doubt, the slightest
discontinuance, a single failure in her armament production
should suffice to ruin Japan. Although imcomparable sen-
timental assets do exist, it may be that in her struggle
Japan has taken aimost too much advantage of such elements.
It is economy that counts, and this factor must be taken
into consideration, especially by Japan which has such
great economical aims. Perhaps that is the big Japanese
stumbling block: They want to follow their Bushido tradition,
the road of the knight, af the Samurai; they still believe
in the Goddess of the Sun who gave her great grandson, the
first of all emperors, the mirror, the diamond chain, and
the sword, thus presenting him with purity generosity and
daring - and nevertheless they aim at establishing the
great Asiatic economic sphere by means of the cold oal-
culating methods of a capitalistic-imperialistic power.
transl: sr

Jω
August
a confidential source, which has furnished reliable information on past
ocoasions, to indicate a plan by the Chilean Government to gather sorap
894.24/1861
94.24
metal throughout Central and South America, with the ultimate purpose
of selling it to the Japanese.
note
It is reported that the Chilean Chargés in the various Central
and South American countries are attempting to purchase this sorap metal
through the medium of third parties and with checks for payment to be
issued by Chilean firms. It is also said that after the sorap metal
has been purchased by the Chilean firms it will be placed aboard ships
of Chilean registry and will then be delivered to Japanese ships after
contact has been made somewhere in the Pacific. Possibly other vital
material besides sorap metals are to be inoluded in the transaction.
It is to be noted that messages concerning the transaction
have not been intercepted by the Canal Zone censor. This is claimed as
another indication that communioations relative to the transaction have
been handled through the Chilean diplomatio pouoh.
This information is being furnished the Coordinator of Inter-
American Affairs.

MEMORANDUM TO THE AMBASSADOR
by Carl H. Boehringer
Reporting Section of the
Embassy at Tokyo.
SUBJECT: Japanese Interest in Chilean Copper.
I have had a lengthy conversation with Guillermo de la Jara,
formerly an attache of the Chilean Legation at Tokyo and a
representative of the Caja de Credito Minero, Santiago, Chile.
This organization is an official concern established to assist the
small copper miners in Chile to dispose of their output. prior
to the outbreak of the ar, Japan purchased most of the copper
produced by these small aines, total shipments during the two
years preceding averaging 5,Ooo tons monthly of copper ore and
2,Ooo tons of copper concentrates. The copper content of the
Chilean ores averaged l2 per cent, while the content of the con-
trates averaged beteen 30: to 40%. In terms of pure copper,
these figures work out at about 1,3oo tons of pure copper a month.
Mr. de la Jara, the source of the foregoing estimates, declared
that the im rtance of the Chilean co er for the Japanese is
actually much greater than is indicated by the figures. ue to
the ,eculiar char cteristics of the Chilean re, mainly the
resence of sul hur, it is es ential to have this ore In Japan
for the refining of domestic low-grade ores, vnich average only
1.5. of pure co: per. A refining technique has been developed in
Japan vhereby the : por domestic ores are mixed ith Chilean ores
and concentrutes and refined rofitaly.
Copper . tocks in Japan are limited and Mr. de la Jara
estimates stocks sufficient only for a period of six months.
Some cop. er ore from the : hili. ines has pecome available to the
Japanese but the ore re,p,ortedly is not high grade and cannot be
used to take the : lace of the Chilean ores and concentrates in
refining operations.
lrior

=== Page 219 === (confidence 0.98)
Japanese offieials from time to thie in
copper situation. They stated that it Will
for Japan to secure copper from Chile and, tha
f1l be taken to assure future supplies. Mr. de la Jar
iven to understand that Japan intends to smash the Panama C
When the Canal has been eliminated as a vital trafric artery
Japan will then feel free to send freighters and escorts to Chile
to get copper. Even if the Canal is not attacked, Mr. de la Jara
was told, Japan may find it necessary to raid the Chilean copper
centers and seize whatever stocks of refined copper may be at hand
When it is remembered that the United States has contracted
for the entire output of the Chilean copper mining industry, the
implications of the foregoing statements will be appreciated.
Mr. de la Jara made it clear that some action by Japan along the
lines indicated may be expected in the near future. Mr. Jorge
Rosselot, former Chilean Consul in Yokohama, confirmed the state-
ments made by Mr. de la Jara and both gentlemen were impressed
with the gravity of the situation and the need to have it called
to the attention of the United States Government.
Mr. de la Jara also stated that the Japanese are well aware
of the fact that American interests dominate the Chilean copper
mining industry. The Japanese are desirous of striking at the
United States by uging Chile to nationalize the copper industry
They point to the saying in Chile that "Los Yankees sacan el cobre
y dejan el hojo" (The Yankees take the copper and leave a hole)
The Japanese have even intimated that they want to help Chile to
drive out "Yankee Imperialism".
According to Mr. de. la Jara, the Japanese haven't much mor
to wait. They need the copper ingots from Chuguioamata,
and al Teniente, where are located ther three-great

=== Page 220 === (confidence 0.96)
-3-
plants operated by two prominent American companies.  They have
their agents in the neighborhood of these plants and the
Japanese Government is kept informed of developments in the
industry. It would seem that their agents might attempt to
commit sabotage or to advise their government of the movement
of copper shipments, which might then be seized at sea. As
previously indicated, the Japanese might also attempt to make
sudden raids on the refineries or warehouses.
Mr. de la Jara added that the Japanese apparently are
casting glances at Easter Island, Chilean possession between
Chile and Australia. A Gaimusho official actually questioned
him closely about the main features of the island, with parti-
cular reference to the availability of fresh water, he said.

CONVERSATICNS BETWEEN THE OHILEAN OCMEROIAL
COUNSELOR AND REPRESENTATIVES OF THE AMERICAN SEOTION OF THE
JAPANESE FOREIGN OFFICE AFTER THE OUTBRKAK OF WAR
Submitted by: Donald W. Lam, Vice Consul, Tokyo, Japan
Mr. Manuel Cuadros, the Chilean Commercial Counselor,
states that in January (1942) Mr. Takata and Mr. Kawasaki, of the
American Section of the Japanese Poreign Office, approached him with
certain proposals in reference to commercial relations between Japan
and Chile. Their propesal at that time was that Valparaiso should
be used as the exchange point instead of Lourenco Marques, and that one
of the large Japanese vessels which would be used for the exohange
would take carge as ballast both ways. Their proposal further contem-
plated the shipment of cement from Japan in return for copper to be
carried on the return voyage. Mr. Cuadros states that he refused to
consider this proposal on the grounds that copper is considered stra-
tegic material and moreover that most of the copper in Chile is held
by American interests. He sent no telegram regarding this conversa-
tion to his heme Govemment.
Mr. Ouadros states that his next contact with these
officials took plac "about the end of May" (1942). This conversa-
tion was initiated by Mr. Cuadros, who states that his idea was
based on trade which had reportedly taken place between the British
and Germans through Shanghai during the present war. This Shanghai
trade was repertedly conducted by Arnhold & Company representing
British interests, and three German firms, Siemens, Melchers, and
Carlewits, representing the German interests. With this situation
in mind Mr. Cuadres suggested to Kawasaki (former Charge d'Affaires
in Chile) and Takate that some trade with Chile might be arranged
involving

=== Page 222 === (confidence 0.97)
impeiving shipments of nitrate to Japan in exehange for rubber.
This suggestion was purely tentative since any arrangement would
have to be made with the agreement of the American authorities.
The two foreign office officials appeared to be interested in this
proposal. After consultation with Japanese Army representatives,
however, they informed Mr. Cuadros that the Army would not consider
such a proposition unless the Chilean Government guaranteed that
this rubber would not be sold to enemy countries, since his pro-
posal visualized the re-sale of such rubber to the United States.
The matter was dropped at this point. However it was arranged that
any further proposals by the Japanese would be made through the
Japanese Minister to Ohile. Mr. Cuadros intends to call on the
Japaneso Minister after his return to Chile, which will take place
about one month after his arrival in Rio. He states that any
further proposals which may be received will be tranamitted to the
American Embassy in Chile.
Donald W.Lam
Donald W. Lamm,
Vi∞ Consul.

Sir:
I have the honor to enclose papers, relating to the
shipment from Mexico to Japan of mercury, molybdenum and
other strategic materials, which would appear to be worthy
of study by concerned agencies of the American and Mexican
Governments.

1/
There will be found in a memorandum of conversation
between a member of my staff and lir. Zavala, former Consul
General at Tokyo of Nicaragua, an account of the manner in
which letters written by Jananese in Tokyo to parties in
Mexico City fell into his hands. Briefly, Mr. Zavala had
assumed charge, with the approval of his Government, of
the Consulate General at Tokyo of El Salvador upon the
departure in October, 194l, of the Salvadorean Consul Gen-
eral.
The letters had been posted at Tokyo at various times
during the three months preceding the outbreak of war in
the Pacific but, ovwing to the suspension of shinping ser-
vices to the American continents, had been withheld by the
Jananese postal authorities. The covers, bearing the legend
"Consul General of El Salvador", ere delivered to Mr. Zavala
for the reason that he was the acting "Consul General of
El Selvador" of record.
He has otherwise no knovledge of,
or part in, the correspondence between the Japanese and the
parties in exico City.
On the other hand, there is strong
evidence, some of it direct, of the involvement of the former
Salvadorean Consul General, r. Siguenza. It should be
stated that Ir. Siguenza,for several years, cut quite a
figure in Tokyo. He maintained an expensive and, having
regard to the narrow streets and gasoline shortage, an
inconveniently large automobile, and otherwise lived in a
manner so ostentatious as to invite curiosity with regard
to the sources of an incone obviously larger than the
stipend which the Government of El Salvador was likely to
pay its Consuls General.
