reference, ir. Zavala informed Lir. Dooman of their substance.

Losure No. 1 to despateh of Ambassader Joseph C. Grew,
ted August 20, 1942, entitled "Shipment from Mexico to

Memorandum:
At the initiative of Mr. Zavala, Consul General of
Nicaragua, I had this morning a conversation with him, of
which the following is the substance:
In October of last year Mr. Zavala was returning from
Shanghai. Somewhere en route, I believe it was on the boat
between Kobe and Yokohama, he received a telegram from
Mr. Leon Siguenza, Consul General of E Salvador at Tokyo,
asking whether Mr. Zavala would be prepared to assume charge
of the Consulate General of E Salvador, Mr. Siguenza having
been ordered to return to his country. Mr. Zavala was aston-
ished by the haste being shown by Mr. Siguenza, and merely
replied that he would return to Tokyo on the following day and
would be prepared to discuss the matter with Mr. Siguenza.
Mr. Siguenza called on Mr. Zavala the next day after the
arrival of Mr. Zavala in Tokyo. He said that if arrangements
could be made for Mr. Zavala to assume charge of the Consulate
General, he would be expected to retain Mr. Siguenza's secre-
tary, buy Mr. Siguenza's furniture, and assume charge of
Mr. Siguenza's child, whose mother was a Japanese woman with
whom Mr. Siguenza had been living. Mr. Zavala replied that
this proposition which he had received was an extraordinary
one, that the question whether he would be prepared to assume
charge of the consulate of El Salvador was a matter which would
have to be referred to his Government; but that in any event he
did not propose to become encumbered with Mr. Siguenza's per-
sonal responsibilities. Mr. Siguenza indicated that in any
event he would like to have Mr. Zavala assume charge of the
consulate, end accordingly the matter was referred to the
Nicaraguan Government which in due course authorized Mr. Zavala
to assume charge of the Consulate General of El Salvador.
Mr. Siguenza then formally notified the Japanese Foreign Office
of the transfer of charge of his consulate to the Consul General
of Nicaragua. Shortly thereafter Mr. Zavala was again approached
by Mr. Siguenza, who said that he had found a Japanese who was
prepared to assume charge of the Consulate General of E Salvador,
to employ the Japanese clerk, and to take charge of Mr. Siguenza's
child, and he asked Mr. Zavala to notify the Japanese Foreign
Office that he ha d transferred charge of the consula te of
El Salvador to that Japanese. Mr. Zavala replied that the
matter was beyond his control, that the interests of E Salvador
had been transferred to him and that he could do nothing further
in the matter without direct instructions from his Government.
There the matter ended so far as Mr. Zavala was concerned. How-
ever, Mr. Siguenza left Japan for the United States in October
but failed to transfer to Mr. Zavala the seal and archives of
the Consulate.
In the course of distributing to the evacuated personnel
on the ASAMA MARU the mail that had accumulated in Japan,
Mr. Zavala received several letters enclosed in envelopes
bearing the seal of the Consulate General of El Salvador in
Tokyo. It is to be recalled that Mr. Zavala was in charge of
the Consulate General of El Salvador up to the time of his
departure from Tokyo. These le tters were addressed to
Abraham Z. Phillips, Calle Londres Num 148, Mexico D.F.,
Mexico.

=== Page 232 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
Mexico. There was also one cover containing a letter dated
October 22 from Mr. Zavala to the Ministry of Foreign
Affairs of San Salvador informing the latter that he had
assumed charge of the Consulate General of E Salvador at
Tokyo. The envelopes addressed to Mr. Phillips contained:
1. A letter dated October 13, 1941 (in English)
from the manager of the Tokyo branch of Petroleos
"Leon" S.A., with translation in Japanese;
2. A letter from the same office to Phillips
dated November 18 (also in English).
3. A letter dated October 5 (in Japanese) from
the manager of the Tokyo branch to Mr. Phillips;
4. A letter dated October 22 (in Japanese) from
the manager of the Tokyo branch to a Mr. Nii, in
care of the head office at Mexico City of the
company.
5. A memorandum dated November 18 (in Japanese)
from the Tokyo office to the head office in Mexico
City.
A careful study will have to be made of the foregoing
papers to extract from them their essention information.
However, the following facts are clear. The Petroleos "Leon"
company has assumed a title which would give the impression
that it deals in petroleum. It was, however, set up as a
blind primarily to provide the Japanese with molybdenum,
copper, mercury, and other primary metals. The letters indi-
cate that some time ago a certain Mr. Joseph (nationality not
indicated) visited Japan and made contact with the Japanese
military authorities through Mitsubishi Shoji Kaisha. Joseph
appears to have been the person to whom the Japanese remitted
large sums of money for payment to Petroleos to cover ship-
ments of the raw materials ordered by the Japanese.
Among certain items of interest is a suggestion by the
Tokyo office of the company to the head office in Mexico
City that ir the latter were unable to ship from Manzanillo
directly to Japan, it might be possible to arrange for ship-
ment on an American vessel to Shanghai, where the goods could
be transferred to the Japanese authorities.
A clipping from the "El Nacional", Mexico City, dated
October 18, 1941, which Mr. Zavala had, reported that there
were indications of mercury having been shipped out of
Manzanillo on a "totalitarian" submarine.
Mr. Zavala, whose honesty and good falth are beyond
susplcion, 1s greatly concerned over a reference in one of
the letters to the fact that the messages communicated from
Japan to Mexico City were being sent through the good offices
of the Consulate General of Er Salvador, for the reason that
he was at the time of writing the acting Consul General for
El Salvador. He has referred all the papers to me for study
and

=== Page 233 === (confidence 0.97)
一3-
and requests that we withhold submitting to the competent
authorities of the American Government any information on

nelosure No. 2 to despatch of Ambassador Joseph C. Grew,
dated August 20, 1942, entitled "Shipment from Mexico to

Memorandum:
The following is a summary of the principal information
contained in the correspondence lent to us by Mr. Zavala,
Consul General of Nicaragua (see Mr. Dooman's memorandum of
August 4, 1942), concerning the activities of Petroleos
"Leon" S.A., a firm with its head office in Mexico City and
a branch in Tokyo, which was engaged in supplying strategic
materials to the Japanese Army and Navy.
The correspondence coming into Mr. Zavala's possession
originated from the branch office in Tokyo and is signed by
the branch manager, a Mr. Fuminaga Fujii (the latest le tter
only, dated November 18, 1941, is signed by a Mr. Y. Nagamatsu
in the absence of Mr. Fujii who, it is stated, had been con-
scribed for military service). Letters dated October 5,
October 13 (two letters), and November 18, 1941, are addressed
to Mr. Abraham Z. Phillips, Managing Director of Petroleos
"Leon" S.A., Calle Londres, No. 148, Mexico, D.F. (the firm's
letterhead gives the following as the address of the head
office: No. 33 Isabel la Catolica, Mexico, D.F., Mexico), and
a letter dated October 22, 1941, is addressed to Mr. Sanshiro
Nii, who is described as a member of the head office in Mexico
City.
These le tters represent that shipments of materials to
Japan were made on board the TAKAOKA, NARITO and SAGAMI MARU
(no dates given), and the HEIYO MARU, which is stated to have
reached Yokohama the middle of September (194l). The nature
of the merchandise comprising those shipments is not indicated.
One further shipment made in late October from Manzanillo,
Colimas, on board the M/S AZUMA MARU is mentioned. It appears
that this last shipment consisted of materials ror the Japanese
Navy, since it is described as having been arranged by
Mr. Joseph, who, as will pe seen from the next paragraph, is
a Japanese Navy representative.
Shipments were not made directly to the Tokyo branch of
Petroleos "Leon" S.A. Those intended for the use of the Japan-
ese Navy were consigned to Mitsubishi Shoji, and those intend-
ed for the use of the Japanese Army are conalgned to Showa
Tsusho, a Japanese firm with its head office in Tokyo. A
Mr. Kobayashi in Mexico City was despatched by the Japanese
naval authorities to represent their interests there. His
duties and powers appear to be of a limited and routine nature,
however, and matters of some importance and negotiations with
the head office of Petroleos "Leon" S.A. are entrusted to a
Mr. Joseph, who is connected with the Japanese Navy and
"returned" (apparently from Japan) to Mexico in July 194l.
A Mr. Ando, acting as the agent in Mexico of Showa Tsusho, is
concerned with purchases made on behabf of the Japanese mili-
tary authorities.
Throughout the correspondence, the head office is urged
to expedite a shipment of 100 tons of 90% molybdenum, the
order for which was placed by Showa Tsusho on behalf or the
Japanese Army authorities and for which payment in advance
(US$134,300) was remitted to Mexico by the Army authorities,
through

=== Page 235 === (confidence 0.99)
through Showa Tsusho, in early July 194l. The urgent need
of the Army authorities for supplies of this mineral, even
in small quantities, is emphasized repeatedly, but there is
no indication that up to the time of the last writing any
definite steps had been taken to efrect shipment from Mexico
of any part of the order. Other items desired by the Army
authorities include electrolytic copper, mercury, asbestos,
gasoline, crude oll, ana lubricating oll. The November 18
letter indicates that the Tokyo branch has been active in
efforts to arrange sales of fuel oil, gasline, and lubricating
oil to the Japanese Army authorities. Mr. Fujii's negotia-
tions in Shanghai with high Army authorities for the sale of
petroleum products are stated to have been abandoned because
of "present difficult conditions," but it is stated that tne
Army autnorltles were prepared to despatch a 10,000-ton tanker
to Mexico to secure oll ii une nead oirice were willing to
cooperate in making necessary arrangements.
Certain materials sent from Mexico had been found unsuit-
able for Japanese Navy and Army use, and it had, therefore,
become necessary to endeavor to dispose of these materials to
private interests. Difficulties and misunderstandings regard-
ing the disposition of and payment for those materials appear
to have led to mutual recriminations between the head office
in Mexico and the Tokyo branch of Petroleos "Leon" S.A., as a
result of which the head office had lost, in large measure,
its confidence in the competence of. its Tokyo branch. It
intended apparently to curtail the latter's activities and
thenceforth to conduct important negotiations with Japanese
Army and Navy representatives in Mexico.
The means employed by the Tokyo branch to avoid censor-
ship of its communications (apprehension is expressed regard-
ing Japanese, quite as much as American and Mexican, censor-
ship) are of interest. In earlier letters the head office
was advised to forward its communlcations (including invoices
covering shipments and related documents) by snip captains for
deposit with a Mr. Fujita, of the Yokohama branch of N.Y.K.
An arrangement, considered even safer,is described in Mr. Fujii's
letter of November 18. Mr. Fujii states that at "very big
expense" an understanding had been reached with the Consul Gen-
eral for El Salvador in Tokyo, whereby the latter was to handle
the firm's letters as diplomatic correspondence, thus render-
ing it immune from censorship in Japan, the United States, and
Mexico. The head office is advised to avall itself of the
mailing facilities of the foreign representative in Mexico,
through whom mail from the Tokyo branch is received, when
despatching communications to the Tokyo branch. Mr. Fujii
states that some charges will have to be met by the head office
in this connection, but expresses apprehension lest the use of
any other channel of communication result in the firm being
blacklisted in all countries.
In his November 18 letter, Mr. Fujii suggests that if no
Japanese vessel calls at Manzanillo to carry materials needed
by the Army authorities, those materials, camouflaged as
materials sent as aid to General Chiang Kai-shek, might be
shipped to Shanghai on American vessels. The Tokyo branch,
it is stated, can effect delivery to the Japanese Army authorities
in

=== Page 236 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
in Shanghai.
The methods employed to finance these shipments were
of a most devious and complicated character, but unfortunate-
ly the available material does not clearly indicate the
channels through which funds were transferred from Japan to
Mexico.
CHARLES A. COOPER
Aboard M/S GRIPSHOLM

While reading through this correspondence my colleague
Mr. Nagamatsu received news of the sudden death of his mother
and he immediately left for his native village in Yamaguchi
Prefecture. His son, Tomoyuki, who was wounded in battle in
central China, recoverd and rejoined the front line troops.
Unfortunately, however, he contractea a local disease, under-
went an operation, and at present is convalescing in a fiela
hospital.
Your letter, received after a long delay told us of your
efforts. Also from Mr. Nii's letter we learned in detail about
conditions in Memico. We were greatly relieved to emerge from
our state of bewilderment resulting from the disruption of com-
munications. In fact, because of ine long suspension of com-

we were very worried that something had happened to you. We
were relieved to learn from your communicatlon that our anxiety
was groundless.
As we were unacquainted with the true conditions affecting
you, in our first and second letters we gave expression to
various complaints whicn we had conjured up. We apologize for
this but please understand that we did it only with a view to
the successful realizatlon of our mutual interests.
As indicated in your letter, under present conditions it
is absolutely impossible to maintain adequate contact by tele-

understand this point and act accordingly.
Next, according to copy of a receipt sent to Mr. Ando of

however, this sum was listed as payment on account of consign-
ment goods. This was completely new and unexpected to both
the contracting parties, this branch office and the military
authorities.
We

=== Page 238 === (confidence 1.00)
We believe, however, that Mr. Ando must have known

believe that the question should be settled under the full
responsibility of Mr. Ando, in other words, Showa Tsusho.
At the time, we (the military authorities and this branch

oil produced in Mexico) will necessarily be greatly facilitated

Due to the effects of the British and American blockade
policy, recently the volume of imports of military supplies into
Japan has been decreasing month by month. The raw materials
which you are shipping to us in accordance with the wishes of
Mr. Joseph (who is connected with the Navy), aside from the
question of volume, ere said to be the best in Central and South
America. Therefore, your ability is highly esteemed.

profit, regardless of any excuses Mr. Joseph may advance for
getting cheap prices. We believe that Mr. Joseph fully realizes
that without your cooperation it is now impossible for him, no
matter where he goes, to fulfill his mission for the purchase
of military supplies.
With regard to the Mitsubishi consignment goods, as we
reported in letter No. 2, this branch office, which is thorough-

infinitely higher than that in Japan), used those prices as refer-
ences/ material in demanding higher prices. A certain section
of the naval authorities is under the mistaken impression that
this branch considers your interests to the exclusion of all else
(this is due to Mitsubishi propaganda). It appears, therefore,
that they are seeking to avoid negotiations regarding price in
order that low prices may be fixed upon in Mexico through
Mr. Joseph.
High-speed steel and speclal steel materials of inferior

which Mitsubishi hopes that the goods will be sold to them.

Summary of letter from Fuminago Fujii (Tokyo) to Abraham Z.
Phillips, Managing Director of Petroleos "Leon" S.A., Mexico
City, dated October 13, 1941.
Inasmuch as there is a big difference in the price you
ask for the high-speed steel and other goods consigned to the
Tokyo Head Office of Mitsubishi Lud. and the present price in
Japan, successful negotiations with the naval authorities are
impossible. Thererore we have drawn up our own price list and
are sending you a copy for reference. At the same time copies
have been submitted to Mitsubishi and the naval authorities
to facilitate decision with regard to price. Upon second
inspection, however, Mitsubishi has brought up an oral complaint
to the effect that approximately 15 tons of the high-speed steel
have been found to be a cheap grade of carbon steel. This
branch office assumes no responsibility for this decision as
the Japan Special Steel Mfg. Co., designated by the naval au-
thorities and Mitsubishi, made the analysis table.
Inasmuch as high-speed steel has been produced in great
quantities in Japan, there is no need to import it. There is
need, however, for cobalt, vanadium and nickel necessary to
the manufacture of high-speed steel.
The naval authorities refuse to touch the aforementioned
useless high-speed steel, so please endeavor to settle this
problem in order to facilitate transactions in the future.
Due to our strong attitude in endeavoring to promote
your interests, Mitsubishi considers us impudent and has even
gone so far as to inrorm the naval authorities that we are
traitors to our natlon. The naval authorities therefore nave
avoided negotiations with us and are trying to open negotia-
tions in Mexico.
In order to cover up the mismanagement of the Taiheiyo
Oil Company which they established last year, a section of
the Navy is trying to make trouble for my colleague, Mr. Naga-
motsu, who at that time opposed and competed witn that firm.
All in all, we have never liked to have transactions with the
Navy. It is o:!y in accordance with Mr. Joseph's wishes that
we have been deling with the Lavy until now. Since you are
in the midst of big transactions with Mr. Joseph, however, for
the time being we will be patient and cautious and exert all
our energies toward managing our end of the business with
regard to the goods you have shipped to us; thereby protect-
ing your interests.
With regard to the goods consigned to the Showa Tsusho
Kabushiki Kaisha we are at present having preliminary con-
sultations with the Army authorities and trying to turn over
the goods to various factories and at the same time price each
item. With regard to these goods, as well as those consigned
to Mitsubishi, however, we have been confronted with various
difficulties because you have not sent us a detailed list or
papers

=== Page 242 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
papers attesting their quality. Particularly, there is a
terrific difference between your invoice prices and the
prices at which the goods can actually be sold.
This firm expects to have large business dealings with
the Army because of our intimate relations with many acquaint-
ances in that Department. For example: supplying the new fuel
oil plant which is to be completed during 1942 at a cost of
600,000,000 yen with various materials, oil, crude oil, lubri-
cating oil, asbestos, molybdenum, etc.
In a telegram from Mr. Nii on October 2, we were informed
that "with the exception of pending business concerning goods
consigned to Mitsubishi and Showa, do not without authority
from the head office touch subsequent shipments. Complications
in transactions are detrimental to the head ofrice". We feel
very badly about this instruction and can hardly believe that
you sent it. Even if you were to ship goods, in accordance with
Mr. Joseph's wishes, Mitsubishi (Tokyo head office) would have
nothing to do with them and therefore our firm wouid have to
carry out all inspectlons of the merchandise, transrers of goods,
in fact everytning connected with their nanaling. For example,
in dealing with huge quantities of merchandise sent to Mitsubishi
and Showa, ve despatched a number of technicians and employees
to handle and watch it. As a result to this day these goods have
neither been lost nor damaged and we are only awalting a price
advance so that we can dispose of them at a price which will
satisfy as nearly as possibly your demand. If, cn the other
hand, these goods were to left to a firm such as Mitsubishi,
all the merchandise might be sold very cheaply, or it might be
lost or damaged in the custom house, or it might be auctioned
or confiscated by the customs.
Even if Mr. Joseph enjoins strict secrecy, it is suggested
that all matters, big or small, be communicated to this branch
by letter or otherwise. Two postcards dated September 8 were
received from Jir. Mii but we could not make out from them what
kind of goods had been shipped nor whether molybdenum had been
put aboard.
Hereafter communicate with us by cable. If that is not
possible, please send messages in care of the ship captain to
be handed to Mr. Fujita, Chief of the General Affairs Section
of N.Y.K., Yokohama branch. He will forward such messages to
us in utmost secrecy. As all foreign mail is to be strictly
censored from October 20, the above procedure should be followed
by both parties concerned.
P.S. Please do not address correspondence direct to
Mr. Nagamatsu in the future but send in care of Mr. Fujita.
Mr. Nagamatsu must be kept in the background or the Navy will
begin exerting pressure on him again.

in the presence of o momher of this branch. Within a few days,
when released by the customs, it vill be dolivered to the naval
authorities in the nane of Mitsubishi Shoji.
Althouch thero are some delays nowadays, there should be no
difficulties. Therefore, please resume the practice of cach
time forwarding conies of invoices ond related documents to cover
mcrchondise shinments. That will enehle this branch to under-
take all clerical wor in the handling of the shinments.
Duo to increased censorshin of cormunications, you are re-
quested to desnatch correspondence (including cases requiring
maximum secrecy) by mail steamer only. Although I emleined
this point in detail to Mr. Phillins in letter No. 2, to save
you trouble, you are informed that the practice of sending
mail for denosit with Mr. Fujita of the Yokohama branch of
Ninnon Yusen Kaisha is discontinued. If mail is posted as do-
mestic after arrival in Janan it is all right. Please bear this
in mind.
On the whole, matters mentioned in your letter of Sentem-
ber 12 are considered reasonable. On two or three points, how-
ever, this branch finds it difficult to agree. You indicate
that the contents of our letters to you are conflicting and
contradictory. However, this branch simply reports to you
straightforwardly and without ulterior motive the progress of
various negotiations. Although this branch has various diffi-
culties, it continues to strive exclusively to advance the
interests of Mr. Phillips and the home office. Matters which
at the moment appear conflicting and contradictory absolutely
cannot

=== Page 247 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
cannot allbe adjusted singlehandedly. The other parties
are shifted and due to changes in their attitudes, it is some-
times impossible to avoid contrariety. It is therefore unjust
to accuse us of bad faith on the basis of appearances only.
Please understand, however, that we will exercise the utmost
care in the future.
You next acouse this branch of commercial incompetence.
I cannot admit that such insulting language requires any reply.
In short, as to whether we are incompetent or whether your
demands (regarding stock disposition) are unreasonable, the
facts are more convincing than hearsay reports. I believe
that if you were to return to Japan you would readily perceive
the difficulty of reconciling the true value of merchandise
held in custom warehouses in Japan with the invoice valuations.
For example, the delay in settling accounts for the Mitsubishi
consignment merchandise is due, for one thing, to the question
of price, since the greater part of the value of this merchan-
dise is accounted for by special steel and unassembled steel
materials of unsuitable quality (because not required by the
naval authorities). The margin of difference between the two
parties is too great, and so long as they do not make conces-
sions, it will be extremely difficult to arrive at a solution.
In my opinion, to ignore this and adhere to the price set forth
in invoice vill render difficult the success of negotiations
anywhere in the world. Moreover, the price of verichrome(?),
as stated in your invoice, is approximately 20 times the Japan-
ese Government's offici liy established price and does not
represent the nrice prevailing anywhere in the world. As a
result, other merchandise, about which there is no question,
is stiil unreleased because of the aforementioned unsettled
merchandise.
Notwithstanding, this branch exoresses its appreciation
for your efforts. In efforts to reduce the price difference
as much as possible, reference material on the high prices pre-
vailing on the Shanghai market were submitted to the naval
authorities and as a result of our insistence in demanding
that they purchase at high prices, with a view to promoting
your interests, we were obliged for a time to keep at a respect-
ful distance from those authorities, as already reported in
another letter to Mr. Phillips, and it was decided by those
authorities to take up the question of price adjustment direct-
ly with the home office in Mexico through Mr. Kobayashi. It
is hoped that you will see in this one instance an indication
of the loyalty to the home office with which we have been
functioning.
Recently, however, there have been personnel changes in
the Navy bureau; officials who formerly were in charge have
been transferred and as a result, although your position is
amply explained in our relations with the new officials in
charge, at this time of inconvenient communication we believe
that settlement of problems by negotiations on the spot with
Mr. Kobayashi, who was despatched by the Navy, is the best
policy for both you and us.
According to your letter, it appears that Mr. Kobayashi
will have nothing to do with the disposition of stock mer-
chandise. Whatever excuses he may offer for refusing to
settle this problem, it is an established fact that the naval
authorities

=== Page 248 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
authorities are to buy up all the aforementioned goods con-
signed to Mitsubishi. Therefore, whether he likes it or not,
Mr. Kobayashi is placed in the position of having to settle
this question in Mexico. Moreover, we consider that it is his
duty to cooperate in settling this matter. This branch is con-
sulting with the naval officer in charge with a view to bring-
ing about an amicable settlement of the question in Mexico.
If you have handed over to ir. Kobayashi as security the
price of shipments on the TAKAOKA, SAGAMI and NARITO MARU, we
fear that the same problem will arise in the future. Although
we have made a detailed examination of and straightened out the
aforementioned goods at Yokohama, and the goods are now stored
in the custom house under guard, we are very concerned because
we expect there to be a considerable discrepancy between the
actual value of those goods and the nrice set forth in your
invoices. Your telegram of October 2, hovever, advised us to
have absolutely nothing to do with the above goods. Therefore,
until you have concluded the transactions which are now in
progress, we consider it important that you demand the sincere
cooperation of  r. Kobayashi to effect full settlement for all
the foregoing merchandise.
With regard to the case of Ir. Ando of Showa, we inforned
you at the outset that he was merely a messenger boy. Later
we cabled that you should accord him the same treatment as
Mr. Koboyeshi, end althouch at first clonce this seens a
reversal of our previous statement, we merely meant that, during
these times of difficult communication, you exnlain to Ir. Ando
in Mexico, for the sake of convenience, the conditions regarding
shipment of molybdenuun.
Previously this branch had no relations with Showe and
accordingly we know nothing of Mr. Ando. According to the mili-
tary authorities and the executives of the Tokyo head office
of Showa, hovwever, Ir. Ando is a mere enployee and even though
sent to Mexico he would be given no authority and would be only
a liaison employee. For this reason it would be only a waste
of time to give too detailed explanations to such an employee
and believing that it would he useless, we telegraphed you at
the time of making the contract.
With the subsequent deterioration in Japanese-American
relations and the changing conditions in your country, tele-
graphic communication, of course, and also mail communication
is gradually becoming more and more difficult. With a view to
compensating for this inconvenience, we cabled you to use
Mr. Ando. Although this may seem incomprehensible to you at
first glance, from our point of view the steps taken in every
case are based on reasonable grounds.
Although reiterated numerous times already, we earnestly
hope that, overcoming all obstacles, the goods under the first
contract with Showa will be loaded. These goods are urgently
needed by the military authorities and the purchase price has
already been paid through Showa. Accordingly, they hope to
obtain the use of the goods as soon as possible.. Because you
have not yet made your first shinment, the Army fuel plant,
for which it is needed, is finding it impossible to manufacture
certain kinds of parts which are indispensable to certain kinds
of

=== Page 249 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
of factory equipment and is encountering great difficulties.
Cognizant of the actual situation, and in view of the
national emergency, we, as Japanese and also as a party to
the contract, wish by some means to get delivery of the afore-
mentioned goods. To the authorities this merchandise repre-
sents only a small sum of money but if it does not arrive,
production will be seriously hampered. After giving thought
to this situation, please effect shipment promptly. At this
time we particularly appeal to your patriotism.
According to receints for Ir. Ando enclosed with your
letter, the price of the foregoing merchandise, $134,300, is
recorded as payment on account of the consignment merchandise.
This, however, is completely new to us. It is most appropriate
that the ahove sum represent the price of the molybdenum, the
cost of the consignment goods beig an entirely separate
matter. If r. Ando has agreed to this, this is not only his
own responsibility but also the responsibility of Showa.
Although we know nothing of this matter, please accord recog-
nition to the fact that the authorities, who actually paid this
sum, made the remittance to Showa for molybdenum. It appears
that you consider that if the molybdenum is not available it
would be all right to anply the money toward payment on account
of the consignment goods. As mentioned in another letter, this
branch has high hones of carrying out large future transactions
with the assistance of those military authorities, but if the
foregoing is put into effect, it is feared that not only will
this ne field be lost but also the future expansion of all
business with the military will be jeopardized. You are urged
to reflect carefully on this point and act judiciously. Other-
wise, in dealing with the disposal of Showa consignment goods,
it will become impossible for our hranch to have any voice in
the matter. That would be most unfortunate.
The foregoing is a frank expression of our feelings and is
offered for your reference. In short, this branch office is
always striving loyally for the expansion of your commercial
interests and is striving to realize your objectives. Please
believe that my colleagues, cur employees and I are all sincere-
ly appreciative of your efforts and that we are coonerating with
you with wholehearted faith. We are eagerly awaiting the day
when, in the neer future, after having settled pending questions,
you will return to Janan.
Fuminaga Fujii, Manager,
Tokyo Branch Office of Petroleos
"Leon" S. A.
P.S. When letters and magazines which we mail reach you, please
send a word of acknowledgment as you have occasion to write us.
If this is not done, we have no way of knowing whether mail
matter reaches you. We would greatly appreciate it if you would
let us know whether you have received the gift which we sent you
the latter part of June.
Lastly, with regard to the contents of communications
addressed to the head office, they are kept completely secret
from such outsiders as Mr. Kobayashi and Mr. Yamaguchi. Please
bear this in mind vhenever you have occasion to have dealings
with those persons.

JAPAN - LEATHER
It has never been possible to calculate Japan's
civil and industrial consumption because it is not known
to what extent the available supplies were required for
the army either for current consumption or for reserve.
In recent years it is estimated that total leather
output has been of the nature of 30,000 tons per annum
with a rising tendency. To split up this figure upon
any authoritative basis is not possible, but the follow-
ing arbitrary division seems not unreasonable.
Industry
5,0oo tons (half of Germany's
estimated pre-war
consumption in this
category)
Armed Forces
15,000
Civilians
5,000
Reserve
5,000
With the spread of the war and its possible extension
to Siberia presumably larger supplies will be required
for the forces and it is suggested that 30,o00 ton
per annum might be required. This figure would look
after the needs of 4 million men on a scale 75% of that
estimated for Germany. It must be emphasised that
Japan has access to the best substitute for leather,
rubber, and therefore, if full supplies could not be
obtained she could go short. Japan's total needs
today may be in the neighbourhood of 40,O00 tons. Nor-
mal surpluses in countries under her control are esti-
mated at:-
Leather content
Japan, Manchukuo, and

able to exploit all her conquests including China to
the full extent of normal exports.

October 17, 1942
London's despatch No. 5602, September 23,
1942 develops the thesis. that the Japanese
are facing political and economic dangers in
the conquered areas because of inability to
supply those areas with manufactured goods
(principally textiles) and because of inability
to buy the raw materials produced in the
occupied areas. This will result in a serious
deterioration of the living standards in the
occupied areas and will probably cause con-
siderable discontent.
he despatch is worth reading in full.

Sir:
ACHE
1942
I have the honor to enclose three copies of
a clipping from The Financial Times (London) of
September 7, 1942, regarding Japanese claims of
production of oil from rubber in Malaya.
Copies
of the clipping have been sent by the Petroleum
Attaché direct to the Petroleum Division of the
Boerd of Economic Warfare and to the Office of
the Petroleum Coordinator in Washington.
NOV - 9 1942

=== Page 269 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
The point of view expressed by the article is,
briefly, that these persistent Japanese claims are
of the nature of propaganda designed to quiet the
apprehensions of the local population resulting
from the loss of their rubber markets. The
article points out that it is technically quite
feasible to make oil fuel from rubber, but
certainly uneconomical in view of the large
The
petroleum resources now available to Japan.
Petroleum Attaché's office here concurs in the
latter view, as does the Ministry of Economic
Warfare.
It is believed that the Japanese emphasis on
the story is evidence of their growing appreciation
of the political and economic dangers to their
position inherent in the nature of the economies
of the conquered areas. Practically all the
regions of southeast Asia overrun by them are
primarily producers of raw materials in large
volume for non-Asiatic markets, the output having
formerly been exchanged for manufactured (chiefly
consumer) goods imported from Japan, India or
western countries. Japan is now in a position
neither to provide a market for their products
nor to supply the necessary goods for their needs.
In these circumstances there appears to be a very
distinct danger, from the Japanese point of view,
that the local populetions (not hitherto antagonistic)
may become discontented and restive.
There have veen other evidences of the growing
uneasiness on this score, as reference to the
following items in the Ministry of Economic Warfare's
weekly economic intelligence notes will show:
Subject
Date
Item No.
4/3/42
24
Fixing of Malayan Rubber
Quota.
5/8/42
33
Survey of Textile Require-
ments of southeast Asia.
5/22/42
26
Substitution of rayon for
cotton in tires for Thailand.
7/3/42
26
Organization of committees
for Price Control in "Greater
East Asia".
27
Readjustment of Agriculture
in the Philippines.
8/7/42
24

exports to Indo-China.
Date/

=== Page 270 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
Date

Subjeot
8/7/42
33

devaluation of the baht.

22

in Thailand.

Plans to continue pro-
duction of rubber, tin
and sugar in N.E.I.
There are also enclosed copies of several DNB
news stories originating in the Far East during renent
months whioh indicate Japanese concern with the pro-
blem of the economic organization of their newly-
acquired territories on a solid basis.
From all these items it is evident that the
Japanese are now keenly aware of the fundamental
nature of their problem. Earlier Japanese theorizing
about the "East Asia Co-Prosperity Sphere" appears to
have taken for granted that all the essential raw
materials could be found in the regions marked out
for their expansion, and that Japan would become
practically the sole supplier of manufactured goods
to them. The Japanese have not previously considered
the problem of disposing of excess supplies of raw
materials, and have apparently been blind to the
fact that the specific raw materials of which Japan
is most urgently in need (except, of course, petroleum)
are not produced in large volume in the regions of
southeast Asia.
The problem may be stated as follows:-
(1) The occupied regions are now unable to
provide themselves with manufactured goods.
(2) A considerable proportion of the manufactured
goods formerly imported by them consists of essential
goods absolutely necessary in the daily lives of the
populations, or necessary to maintain even a re-
stricted output of their existing industries. Of
this class of goods perhaps textiles are the most
important, the proportion of normal imports (by
value) represented by this item ranging from about
10% to 35% in the various countries involved.1
Japan/
1
These peroentages have been derived from International
Trade Statistios, 1938 League of Nations Economio
Intelligence Service, Geneva, 1939.

=== Page 271 === (confidence 0.99)
(3) Japan, apart from her shipping difficulties,
herself is suffering from a marked shortage of con-
sumption goods. This shortage is most acute in the
case of cotton textiles, and Japan now has access
to only very small supplies of raw cotton.
(4) In order to supply even a part of the
demand for these goods in the newly-acquired
territories the Japanese will have to reduce further
their own consumption of them.
(5) If Japan does reduce home consumption the
growing shortage at home will almost inevitably be
refleoted in internal price levels and in the price
struoture of her economy, accentuating the existing
strains and stresses.
The situation appears likely to have the
following economic effects:
(1) In the first stage of Japanese exploitation
the terms of trade will be forced down against the
populations of the occupied regions, since they will
have to exchange very large surpluses of raw materials
for the very restricted quantity of manufactured goods
Japan can supply.
(2) In order to avoid as far as possible the
inflationary effect on her internal economy of the
added demand of the occupied territories, Japan will
force down the exchange rates against the currencies
of the occupied regions, or substitute wherever
possible the military yen (not legal tender in Japan).
Evidence of some such policy has already appeared in
the devaluation of the Hong Kong dollar and the baht.
(3) The exchange policy will be backed by
strict rationing of exports from Japan. There is
evidence that this is already being put into practice.
(4) As time goes on, the living standards of
the populations in the occupied territories will
deoline, and as the terms of trade go against them
they will gradually turn from the produotion of goods
for export to Japan to the production of goods for
their own needs. This does not imply the building
of modern factories in those regions, but merely the
revival of handicraft industries, whioh the Japanese
can do little to stop, but which will inevitably
reduce considerábly the labor supply for other
industries. The fact that so large a proportion of
the present labor supply, e.g. of the mining and
rubber industries in Malaya, consists of immigrant
Chinese and Indian labor, will also make it hard for
the Japanese to prevent them from shifting to other
industries or even migrating away from the countries
of southeast Asia.
(5)1

=== Page 272 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
(5) Notwithstanding all this, there will
still remain very important deficiencies in the
supply of essential goods, of whioh the most
important will probably be the shortage of textile
goods.
Thus in the first stage Japan is likely to
get relatively large quantities of raw materials
at a relatively low cost, if she can provide the
shipping to move them. As time goes on she will
get less and less, while discontent and unrest in
the occupied regions are likely to grow.
Careful consideration of the foregoing argument
suggests that the critical difficulties in Japan's
economic position in relation to the conquered
territories (apart from her shipping difficulties)
are (1) the lack of adequate supplies of raw cotton,
and (2) the inability of her industries to turn out
goods for war consumption and goods for civilian
consumption in large volume at the same time.
With respect to the problem of organizing the
recently-acquired regions, the shortage of goods
other than textiles may restrict Japan's ability
to maintain the existing capital equipment of their
existing industries and to make the necessary new
investment to develop the regions in accordance with
her plens for "Greater East Asia"; but the writer
does not believe it will cause her serious difficulty
with the local populations. The peoples of those
regions can, and probably will, resort to other
means of meeting their needs, or do without altogether.
However, the demand for textile goods must be met,
since clothing is certainly one of the most essential
needs of human life; and historically textiles have
formed the principal class of goods imported for
consumption in those regions. It is unlikely that
large stocks existed there when the Japanese came in,
so that the shortage will probably become acute in a
rather brief period of time.
The Japanese can meet the demand for textiles
in three ways: (1) By the use of substitute fibers,
e.g., silk, rayon or staple fibers; (2) By the
acquisition of new sources of cotton supply, e.g.,
China or India; (3) By the encouragement of local
production in the regions of southeast Asia. It
appears unlikely that the first method would prove
successful, as the supply of silk is probably too
small, and the rayon and staple fiber industries
draw heavily on both pulp and chemical supplies
which are probably already becoming short. The
conquest of India would require time and extensive
military operations, though it is not unlikely that
the attempt may be made; but the pacification of the
North/

=== Page 273 === (confidence 0.98)
6
North China cotton-growing regions has already proved
an insoluble problem for the Japanese. The pro-
duction of cotton in the countries of southeast Asia
seems to be limited by unfavorable climatic conditions,
but the possibility of resort to other fibers, e.g.,
ramie, should not be overlooked. Probably the
Japanese will try all methods but it hardly seems
likely a priori that they will find a satisfactory
solution without gaining access to better sources
of supply of raw cotton.
In any case, the broad conclusion is that there
will be a sharp and serious deterioration of the
living standards of the populations of the occupied
regions which will probably cause considerable
discontent and unrest emong them. Such a situation
should provide a fertile field for anti-Japanese
propaganda.
The main lines of the preceding analysis have
been discussed with officials of the Ministry of
Economic Warfare here and they agree that it is
substantially correct. In this connection reference
should be made to the Ministry's six-monthly survey
(Report No. 20), issued June 30, 1942. However, it
is believed advisable to pursue a more detailed
quantitative analysis of the problem, based on
recorded trade figures for ell the countries involved,
before the conclusion can be accepted as definitely
valid; and the material for such a study is not
available in this office.
Respectfully yours,
For the Ambassador:
James Somerville
First Secretary of Embassy,
Economic Warfare Division.

JAPANESE PLANS IN EAST ASIA. TOKIO, JulY 30, 1942
Hinsichtlich Japans Massnahmen in Wirtschafts
Raum von Grossostasien lassen sich fuenf Hauptstufen
der japanischen Planung erkennen. Im Vordergrund
steht die Vereinheitlichung der Weehrungs - und
Investitionspolitik. Instryment der Waehrungs-
politik ist der Militaer-Yen.I
Der Yen selbst
zirkuliert nur in Japan; der Militaer-Yen ist
gesetzliches Zahlungsmittel in Burma, Malaya,
niederlaendisch Indien, Hongkong, Echanghai und
auf den Philippinen. Die Waehrungspolitische
Einheit der Gesamtzone wird vorlaeufig durch der
Verrechnungsverkehr der japanischen Zentralbank
mit hen einzelnen Gebieten hergestellt. Die
Finanzierung des Wirtschaftsaufbaus erfolgt in
den einzelnen Gebieten durch besonders
Finanzierungsgesellschaften, und zwar in
Anlehnung an die Erfahrungen der "Mandschurischen
Gesellschaft fuer Sohwer-Industrie" von 1937 und
der "Entwicklungsgesellschaft fuer Nordchina".
Als zweiter Punkt der Planung ist der Ausbau
des Verkehrswesens zu nennen. Auf dem Festlande
steht die Vereinheitlichung der manschurischen und
Nord-und Mittelchinesischen Bahnsysteme in

JAPANESE OIL
FROM
RUBBER PLAN
Claims Discredited
LONDON EXPERTS
SCEPTICAL
London experts, official and un-
offieial. are satisfied that Axis oil-
from-rubber claims contain far more
propaganda than oil. According to
Japanese broadcasts. large-scale
plans are being made to produce oil
from the surpius rubber of Malaya
and N.E.I. For transport reasons
this may present an advantage to
the Japanese.
In areas where rubber is on the spot
while oil has to be imported by incon-
venient routes threatened by the Allied
Navies and Air Forces. oil (especially
fuel-oil) from rubber might be handy for
:he Japanese. Bu: there are strong
grounds for believing that it is not, and
has no prospect of being. other than a
very minor matter.
When rubber was at bargain prices in
the early thirties. it was not uncommon
in Malaya for planters to run their cars
and rubber milling plants on oil distilled
from rubber in home-made brick-built
stills. Rubber provided both the con-
ton: of the sti!l and the fuel for it. It
is thought that the Japanese claims rest
on no more than the reopening of some
of these stills. The limitations on any
major commercial development are very
formidable.
The heavy chemical plant required is
not available in Malaya or N.E.I. and
cannot be brought there by the Japanese
without more trouble than the process
seems worth. In any (ase. the Japanese,
according to London rubber techno-
logists, have not got rubber to burn in
either Malaya or N.E.I. Rubber in the
trees is not the same as rubber in
Japanese hands.
Translating the one into the other re-
quires materials suc as coagulants
(formic and acetic acids) which must be
imported; an organisation of labour; and
reconstruction f the rubber estates'
plant.
Although the rubber trees did
not lend themselves to scorched earthing.
the smoke houses and milling plants were
extensively destroyed.
These obstacles do not prevent the
Japanese fully satisfying their require-
ments of rubber as such. but they are not
likely to have any large surplus for
inefficient synthetic processes.
AN OLD IDEA
The technology of oil from rubber is
unquestionably inefficient. It has been
known for about 60 or 70 years. and any
talk of "new invent.ons" is off the
mark. In 1933 a British engineer ran
his car on motor spirit distilled from old
rubber tyres and other scrap rubber.
During the early thirties the Inter-
national Rubber Regulation Committee
experimented with distilling surplus
rubber for various purposes in the hope
of finding some alternative to restricting
the output of rubber.
À tar for romd
surfacing promised some success, but
the oil distiliation proved commercially
hopeless.
Even costing the rubber as
free. it worked out eight or ten times as
expensive as natural oil.
Low temperature carbonisation of
rubber yielda-in laboratories-about
35 per cent. of a tar which may be dis-
tilied into three fractions correaponding
roughly to motor spirit (5 per cent.).
Diesel oil (20 per cent.) and fuel oil (10
per cent.). The motor spirit fraction is
of high resin content and does not
respond readily to any normal reming
trentment. It aiso has à low octane
number.
The Diese! nil has poor cotene and
conradson value. and some samples were
found to attack metals. Only the fuel-
oil fractien, either alone or mixed with
Diesel ol. was found to have fairly
satisfactory properrics.
Accordng to how far she succeede in
rehabilitating the rubber plantations.
Japan will dispese of an annual rubber

México, September 13, 1942
Strictly Confidential
AMERICAN EMBASSY
Dear Philip:
I have your letter of August 31 with which you were
good enough to send me a strictly confidential despatch
dated August 20, 1942 prepared by Ambassador Grew on the
Gripsholm, entitled "Shipment from Mexico to Japan of Stra-
tegic Materials." I note that this information is being
currently indexed and digested in the Department and it is
very good of you to give this information to me without de-
894.24/1872
lay. I have noted the further comment which you make in
the second paragraph of your letter with regard to Señor
Zavala Urtecho.
I read this despatch and its enclosures very carefully
last evening. 1 find that it refers to transactions which
x7
a man named Phillips, a naturalized Mexican citizen here,
had with the Japanese and for which he was placed on our pro-
claimed list. Phillips is an enterprising and very intelli-
gent Jew and, like many others here and in other countries,
including our own, found no inconvenience in making money out
2
of dealing with the Japs. The Japanese, of course, were very
890
much interested in getting strategic metals and scrap iron
out of Mexico. Phillips was undoubtedly one of the men en-
gaged in this traffic here. Before December 7, 194l, he
P
considered it entirely proper. Already, before December 7,
S
S/TL
the Mexican Government had certain regulations with regard
to the export of certain metals and so far as I can learn
from our records here, and we have followed the matter very
closely, Phillips stopped having anything to do with these
shipments to Japan as soon as they became illegal under the
Mexican law, and this was already before December 7 of last
year. You will note that the enclosures to Ambassador
Grew's despatch are dated August, September, and October of
1941 and have to do with shipments and transactions before
that time. In other words, the enclosures to Ambassador
Grew's despatch show clearly that reference is made to past
business
0
FILE
20
Philip W. Bonsal, Esquire,
0194
Department of State,
Washington, D.C.

=== Page 281 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
business rather than to future business or current business
and the preoccupation is to clear up these transactions.
We have followed this whole matter very closely here
because there is a great deal of feeling here in certain
places in the Government that Phillips, although he was en-
gaged in these transactions with the Japanese, was only
do ing what many in the United States were doing and that as
soon as, under our law and Mexican law, this trade was no
longer legal and proper, Phillips got out. The facts seem
to show this. In any event, it is certain that the Japanese
had so involved Phillips in these transactions that in the
end he lost more on these transactions than he had made when,
because the Japanese knew the war was certain, some of the
later shipments were not paid for. In other words, the Japs
outsmarted Phillips.
I have, of course, no sympathy with any individual who
traded with the Japanese during any part of 194l. On the
other hand, there are lots of people in the United States en-
joying favor and who are our own citizens and who are current-
ly doing business with our Government, who engaged in exactly
the same kind of transactions that Phillips engaged in with
the Japanese. In other words, he is neither blacker nor
whiter than many of these people at home who traded with the
Japanese in exactly the same way and who are now doing busi-
ness with our own Government at home, or otherwise normally.
The only thing which concerns me about Ambassador Grew's
despatch is that, while all that is covered in his despatch
and its enclosures is water under the bridge and is a closed
matter in fact, it is going to reopen the case against
Phillips here, who is on our proclaimed list, and I see yet
no reason why he should be kept on our proclaimed list or put
on the Mexican proclaimed list. The Mexicans will certainly
not wish to put him on their proclaimed list when it is quite
clear that he ceased these transactions with the Japanese
when they were no longer proper under Mexican law and ours.
The ot her thing which concerns me about it is that, now
that this information in the despatch of Ambassador Grew is
being indexed, etcetera, we are going to have the question
of mercury raised again. There is no doubt that there were
shipments of mercury to Japan and other destinations from
Mexico last year and that it was a profitable business. We
have succeeded in stopping this exportation of mercury ex-
cept to the United States. There is a fairly good article
on this matter in Harper's Magazine for September, 1942.
In/

=== Page 282 === (confidence 0.99)
IAIL
-3-
In spite of the fact that these shipments of mercury to
destinations other than the United States have been stopped
and we are getting all the exportable mercury from Mexico,
this question keeps coming up again and again and various
agencies of ourGovernment seem to have plenty of time to go
into this matter. Some time ago the Navy expressed a new
interest in this and felt that mercury was still being
shipped to Germany from here through German submarines. This
is utterly fantastic. My Military Attaché tells me that he
has instructions, too, to go into this matter. It seems to
me they have more important things to do.
In Mr. Ransom's office, which is responsible for the
procurement of metals from Mexico, and in the Embassy, we
have as accurate information as it is possible to get with
regard to the production of mercury and we know that we are
geiting this production. If there is smuggling of mereury
now, it is confined, in my opinion and in that of others
who know here, to very small quantities which may be smuggled
across our own borders into the United States so as to es-
cape the Nexican taxes. It gets, however, into our hands at
home.
I am only giving you these observations in a preliminary
way, as I fear that this despatch of Ambassador Grew's may
have only the effect of stirring up a lot of our people at
home on a chapter which is really closed. I shall of course
have the despatch and its enclosures carefully studied by our
people here and if there are any further useful observations
to be offered, I shall transmit them to the Department. I
wanted you, however, to have this preliminery information
without delay, as I hope we may be able to stave off a lot
of unnecessary activity on the part of various agencies of
our Government which will only take up their time at home and
ours here without any really useful effect, when we have so
many important things to do at home and here.
I appreciate very much your sending me this preliminary
information.
With all good wishes,
Faithfully yours,

Mexio0, September 18,1942
My dear Dri Bateman:
I am in receipt of a telegram from the Depart-
ment no. 1251 of September 15, 11 p.m., for Mr. Ran-
som from you, as follows:
"The question of smuggling
mercury out of Mexioo has again come up. will
you kindly summarize all information avatable
to you to date and airmail."
As Mr. Ransom is in Washington and I am sure in
contact with you, I am not sending a copy of this tels-
gram to Mr. Ransom's office for the present but shall
hohd this up until he returns here. I am a good deal
concerned about this continued oropping up of the mer-
oury matter. My Military Attaché and Naval Attaché have
had instruotions from their respective Departments to go
into this matter of meroury amuggling and I have told
them that I am oonfident that there is no smuggling ex-
cept the possibility of amall amounts aoross the Mexican
border into the United States in order to evade the Mexi-
can taxes. Whatever this movement is across the border,
it is very small and in any event it goes into our own
country.
For almost all the thirty years that I have been
in the Foreign Servioe, I have been one of those who
have watched smuggling into the United States and smug-
gling aotivities in general on behalf of our Treasury
Department, whioh has always had an interest in these
matters. Whils I was in Belgium for some years I became,
I believe, somewhat of an expert on this matter, as the
muggling of diamonds from there into the Unit ed States
was a problem we had to deal with constantly. Being small
in bulk and great in value, there was naturally an in-
centive to smuggle, and I oan assure you that with the
best efforts that foreign officials and our owm expert
Treasury offioials put forward, it was almost impossible
completely to stop the muggling of diamonds. I think
it
Dr. Alan M. Bateman,
Chief, Metals and Minerals Division,
Board of Eoonomio Warfare,
Washington, D.C.

=== Page 287 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
it will be almost impossible for us, similarly, to
stop the smuggling of small amounts of meroury from
Mexico into the United Statss over the border. The
shipments would obviously be small in volume and there
is a fair incentive for amuggling. I do not believe
that this is on a scale to give us concern, especially
as the meroury gets into our own market. We have no
direct evidence that there is such smuggling of small
amounts across the border, but I think it is a fair
assumption that small amounts are being smuggled in
that direotion.
I think there is no foundation whatever, and we
have not been able to get any, that meroury ts being
smuggled out of Mexioo through German submarines or
in any way that it will reach the enemy. There is
naturally a lot of talk about this, just as there is
about a lot of other things, but when one comes down
to it we md that there is no foundation to these ru-
mors.
It is possible that certain amounts of meroury
have Ween shipped to the Argentine, and this is what
we are going into. If any is going there, I would
not doubt the possibility of its ultimete destination
being to the ememy. Aside from possible shipments to
the Argentine, I cannot find any foundation whatever
for any of these rumors.
All these rumors and reports wi th regard to the
smuggling of meroury are to a large extent based upon
what happened months ago and before we went into the
war. It is possible that this question has arisen
again because of a despatoh whioh Ambassador Grew,
formerly in Japan, wrote to the Department of State on
August 20, 1942, while he was on board the GRIPsH0L,

with regard to this in a striotly confidential letter

will be glad to make a copy of it available to you.In
this letter to Mr. Bonsal I called attention to the
fact that Ambassador Grew's despatoh refers to trans-
axtions which took place last year and which stopped
before our entry into the war.
One of the difficulties we have is that people
coming hown here fron hete hear these rumors with re-
gard to the smuggling of meroury, as they hear other
rumors, and they send these reports home, whioh of
course give concern. Even some of the agents of our
own Government - persons of most of whom the Embassy
is not aware when they are in Mexico - send in irres-
ponsible reports wi th regard to meroury and other
matters.

=== Page 288 === (confidence 0.99)
-3
matters. They hear these rumors and send back these
reports and they are given a credence which they should
not have.
I need not assure you that we are very much inter-
ested in following this matter and we would be the
first to give any information to our Government with
regerd to impmoper shipments of meroury. I am sure that
if there were any improper shipments of meroury the Mexi-
can Government under present conditions would be the first

The Neutral1ty Aot of 1935, as amended in 1937, gave
the Presideat amthority to prohibit the export to sommtries
whieh he might designate as belligerents of "arms, ammeml-
tien, and implements of war". The ast provided that "The
arms, ammunition, and implemeats of war so enumerated shall
imelude those enumerated in the President's proolamatien
mambered 2163, of April 10, 1936, but shall not 1nelude
raw materials or any other articles or materials not of
the same general character as those enumerated in the said
preelamation, and in the Convention for the Supervision of
the Imternational Trade in Arms and Ammunition and in
Implememts of War, signed at Geneva, June 17, 1925." The
term "arms, munition, and implements of war" thus inoluded
artieles such as riflef, machine guns, ammunition, tanks,
vessels or war, airoraft, et cetera. The term did not
1nolude raw materials or sorap iron or petroleum produots.
During the first eight months of 1939, before the war
broke out in Europe, public opinion asserted itself against
any alteratlon of the so-oalled Neutrality Aot of 1935, as
amonded 1n l937, that would permit the export of arms,
ammunition, and implements of war to countries designated
by the President as belligerents. Following the outbreak
of war 1n Europe, the Act of 1935, as amended, was repealed
by the Aot or November 4, 1939, under whioh the prohibition
against the export of arms, ammunition, and implements of
war to countries designated as belligerents wss no longer
maintained.
Ne1ther the Act of 1935, as amended, nor the Aot of
1939 oontained prov1sion for making discrimination between
opposing belligerents except es applied to American repub-
lies. Under the arme embargo 1t was not possible to deny
exports to Japan without at the same time deny1ng exports
to China.

sent to the Japs, which is now being hurled back in
the form of shells at our soldiers. The public has not
forgotten the slackmess somewhere along the line, which
caused the tragedy at Pearl Harbor, the senseless loss
of lives, and material.
The publie expects its offieials who have or should have
all the information to be ahead of the onemy, not wait
until the enemy strikes. The fact that a soldier a1l
sacrifice his life when ordered to do so, does not make
him expemdable because of lack of foresight or acumen
in those with whom we entrust the dispatch of the duties
of government.
Nor has the public forgotten the sacrifice of the Phillipines
was the public at a disadvantage or was the government? If
the goverament was at a disadvantage, then why was it?
Respectfully,
Hararighh
894.24
Harold O. Wright.

Department of State

No. 139
MEMORANDUM OF THE PRESS CONFERENCE, TUESDAY, OCTOBER 27, 1942
INDIA
A correspondent asked if the Secretary would care to
make any comment on some of Mr. Willkie's references to our
foreign policy, particularly his reference to our policy
toward India. Mr. Hull replied that he did not think there
was much for him to add to what he had said to the corres-
pondents in the past. Some Americans, he said, have something
to say every day very naturally on some phase of the British-
Indian situation and they have varying views. Mr. Hull told
the correspondent that he thought he had made clear to the
press on two or three occasions the attitude of this Govern-
ment during this last period when that question had been more
under discussion than heretofore and he repeated that this
Government is, of course, very much interested in that situ-
ation. The Government, said Mr. Hull, is closely observing
the develonments and is watching opnortunities to give the
fullest attention feasible to the situation. Mr. Hull pointed
out that he could not go into the details and that is where
the critic has the advantage at the moment over those of us
who are charged with the responsibility of conducting our
foreign affairs. Mr. Hull said that he might add that for
some time, especially during his stay at the State Department,
the Department has proclaimed and carried forward both in
preachment and in practice what has been believed to be in all
respects forward-looking policies and practices in accorâance
with them; that we have been doing so progressively; that we
are content to continue in those ways in the future. Whilo
wo would not expect condemnation altogether, which was not
good, Mr. Hull said on the other hand, he bolieved we would
havo a diminishing amount of criticism as our record was un-
folded and the full facts as we go forward were moro clearly
understood. Mr. Hull emphasized that he was not speaking
about Mr. Willkic or any other person in particular but was
just making a general remark.
MARTINIQUE
In reply to a question as to whether there were any
new dovelopments in the Martinique situation, the Secretary
said ho thought that soon he would be able to give the cor-
respondents a final summing up on that subject.
ECUADOR
A correspondent asked if the Secretary cared to comment
on the announcement from Ecuador that the President of
Ecuador would shortly visit the United States. Mr. Hull
said that Ecuador and the United States have long been on
especially good relations from the standpoint of understand-
ing and confidence and trust in each other and the spirit of
friendly cooporation both botween themsclves and as membors
of the Pan American Union. Mr. Hull added that the prcsent
Prosident was one of the able statesmen of that part of the
world and ospecially typifies and personifies the ideas and
coaparative policics and programs of the American Republios
and we were looking forward with keen interest to his coming.
He would, of courso,be most weloome, Mr. Hull conoluded.
M. J. MoDermott.

RA - Mr. Bonial:
Information of undoubted reliability discloses that
the Japanese are trying to work out arrangements to secure
copper in Chile, export it to Argentina, and re-export it
to Japan. A Chilean company called de la Jera is being
considered by the Japanese to handle the deal.
894.24/1880
Would you please: (1) have someone look into the
feasibility of this operation and (2) post our Embassies
at Buenos Aires and Santiago.

of November 19, no. 1404.
I wish to report that an investigation indicates that
"Delajara" is a certain "Guillermo Delajaramont", a partner
of Ovalle Delajara y Cie, llll Agustinas, Santiago, Chile.
In 1939 this man was sent to Japan as the sales manager of the
Cajada Credito Minera. While in Japan he sold directly to the
(y)
Japanese Government. When sales of Chilean metal to Japan
were stopped by the war in the Pacific, Delajaramont became
an attache of the Chilean Embassy in Japan. He was evacuated
on the steamship GRIPSiOLM.
The Department is informed that the first steps in the
proposed transaction namely, the exportation of Chilean copper
to the Argentine, is feasible of course. Undoubtedly our Embassy
at Buenos Aires will report whether it is possible to reexport
copper from Buenos Aires.to Japan.
The above message hes been repeated to Buenos Aires for
the information of our Embassy there.

My Aoar Mrs. Hudson:
Your letter of November 16, 1042, addreseed to the
President, in whioh you inquire whether "the United
States is still selling sorap to Jepan", has been
referred to this Department.
On July 2, 1940 legal suthority for restrioting ex-
ports to Japan was granted by Congress. Under that
authority a large number of strategic commodities, in-
N
oluding certain grsdes of scrap iron, were in rapid
suocesslon made subjeot to a licensing system and
licenses for export to Japan werc penerally denied.
On Cotober 1e, 1940 all grades of iron and steel sorap
were placed under the llooneing system, and from thot
date all licenses for shipment or iron and steel scrap
of any type to Japan were denied.
On July 26, 19l, following Japan's military move
in south Indoohina, ell Japaness assets in the United
States were frozen, resulting in a practical cessation
of trade between this country and Japan. All commeroial
interoourse between the two oountries completely oessed,
of oourse, with the outbreak of war.

"Hero without Headlines", quoting from Drew Pearson's Blue
Network Broadoast, October 1l - "The War Department
tonight I want to nominate a Herc ithout Headlines, who in
very quiet way did a great job for his country. He is Gen-
eral Julian Hatcher of Army Ordnance, who some years ago
at Frankford Arsenal developed a new tracer bullet which
lights up its target, and shortly afterward was astonished
to receive orders from the State Department to turn this
seoret over to an American company for sale to Japan. This
was an official order. But for once General Hatcher
violated an official order. He had another secret formula
for a tracer bullet, developed some months before; and
without telling anyone, he gave'the old formula to the
Japanese. I now nominate as a hero without headlines,
General Julian Hatcher, who gave the Japanese this old
tracer bullet. formula which was perfectly good except for
one thing - after the cartridges have been stored for six
months, they explode".

DIVISION OF FAR SASTERN ABEAIRS
January 19, 1943.
UNCLAS
The situation described in this despatch
1s a very complex and difficult one. Among
the factors involved are (1) the internal
political relationships within China, (2) the
question of the maintenance in particular
areas by the Chinese Government of its pol1-
tical and military authority, and (3) the
question of the Chinese Government monopolies
and the ramifications thereof. Whether some
helpful steps might be taken by this Govern-
ment 1s a matter calling for most careful
study. It would seem desirable that any step
taken should be along informal and perhaps
indirect lines. One possibility would be to
mention the matter orally and informally to
Dr. T. V. Soong when he returns to Washington.
Another possibility would be that any special-
1sts sent to China.by the BEW for the procure-
ment of strategic materials might tactfully
use their influence toward bringing about an
1mprovement in the situetion reflected in the
attached despatch. It is believed, however,
that any such approach by BEW representatives
should be undertaken only after the most
thorough discussion of the matter with the
American Ambassador at Chungking.
It is suggested that the interested of-
fices and officers of the Department keep the
foregoing observations in mind with a view to
giving further study to the matter.
A FE:MMH:HES

UNCLASSIFIED
WThe Honorable
The Secretary of Stato,
Washington, DAN??1
E
the Department, copy of a confidential memorandum sub
mitted to me by Mr. John S. Service, Third Secretary of
Embassy, reporting observations during his recent trip
to the Northwest, on the subject of Chinese trade with
the Japanese in strategic materials.
394.24/1
Mr. Service found that there is a large trade in
wool from Ningsia to the Japanese in Suiyuan. Until
recently, wool from West Kansu and Chinghai appears also
to have gone into Ningsia and thence to Suiyuan to the
Japanese but now apparently that trade has been stopped.
Cotton is going from Honan eastward across the Yellow
C
River to Japanese occupied territory, while tung oil also
0
freely reaches Japanese hands. Bristles are obtainable
G
by the Japanese, but probably not the best bristles from
Szechuan.
While wolfram is not produced in the areas visited
by Mr. Service, he traveled to the northwest with a party
of National Resources Commission officials, riding a truck
carrying wolfram for Russia. His comments on the wolfram
situation are based in part on the information obtained in
casual conversations on the subject with these officials.
I agree with lir. Service's observation that an import-
ant factor in the situation reported is the operation of
purchasing monopolies which restrict the earnings of the
actual producer, but add substantial overhead and profit
for their own benefit. Under these circumstances it is not
surprising that the producer may be induced to divert his
production to channels by which it may return him higher
S
prices.
Reppectfully yours
CLAS
