JANDUM FOR THE AMBASSADOR
November 10, 1942
Subject: Chinese Trade in Strategic Materials
with Japan.
The infomation givem below comes in almost every case
from beth Chinese and foreign sourees. There is no doubt of
the mevement of the materials oneerned: it is a matter of
commen knowledge. But volume, mamamr of finanoing, and
extent of ofricial approval or participation are, of course,
impessible to ascertain.
That Japan has been able to obtain strategic materials
produced in the oseupied and guerrilla areas is well known -
beyond initial sabotage of mines, little has ever been done
to hinder Japanese exploitation. It also appears that Japan
is being permitted to obtain several strategio materials
from the unoccupied portion of China. These include: wool,
cotton, tung oil and tungsten (wolfram).
The first two are needed by China for its own war
offort. Demestic supplies are insufficient and wool is
being supplied to Russia. The result is suate shortage
whieh affects even the woelon tairmy blaaket'program of the
Chinese Industrial Cooperatives. Cotton cloth is one of
China's greatest needs. Tungsten is of vital importance
to the United States and its allies.
But the loss of these materials to the United Nations
may not be as important as the effeet of their falling into
Japanese hands. China is now the only souree of all four
of them whieh is not being denied to Japan.
Woo1
There is a large trade in wool frem Ningsia Province
to the Japanese in Suiyuan.
Before the war Ningsia was the largest producer in
China of sheep and eamel wool. At presemt none of this-
wool is go ing south into Kansu to the Chinese Government
momopoly orgamisation. Only a little gees to the Com-
munists in North Shensi. It must be assumed that the
trade with Suiyuan consumes the great bulk.
The export of weol is prohibited by the Chinese
Goverament. But Central Gevermment control is weak im
Ningsia Provinee and the Maritime Custems has not yet
been able to fumetion there.
The weel at presemt is collested along the Yellow
River near the oity of Ningsia and transported overland
by saraven from a large new trading center on the south
batk (wu Chim Tu?) to Paotew, the railhead. Trading is
astually by Mehamedan mershants but conditions in the
provinee are sueh that the Chairman, General Ma Hung-kuei,
is consi dered si ther the owner of partner of all these
firms. They esnnet do business Wi thout his appreval and
wi thout giving him a share in profits.
U11

=== Page 352 === (confidence 0.98)
-2
Until recently wool from Chinghai Province and West
mmu, both sovermmd by semi-independent Mohammedan
semerals, also went out to Suiyuan through Ningsia. With
the expulsion of the general in West Kansu, the Central
Government monopoly is new operating there. This has
bloeked the avenue of shipment from Chinghai also, and
the Chairman of that province is now turning over its
wool, gathered by his own private monopoly, to the
Central Government.
Gotton
Cotton from Honan is going east aoross the Yellow
River into occupied territory.
Honan, before the war, was the third or fourth pro-
ducer of otton in China. Since then, due to greater
war needs for food crops, planting has been redueed
al though o∞tton remains an important orop. Quantities
shipped west into Free China have been relatively mall
because of oommunication difriculty. There 1s no local
manufacture bxeept handioraft production. The balanee,
a large proportion of the orop, must be assumed to be sold
in occupied territory. with this year's orop only about
30% of normal, I was informed at Loyang that some is none-
theless going out.
The traffic is carried on privately on a mall soale.
It is part of the tremendous smuggling trade in both
direotions aoross the Yellow River in Honan. This is
supposed to be under government control - to prevent import
of certain luxuries and the export of strategic materials
(such as ootton). But onditions are bad because a smug-
gling trade is by its nature diffioult to control, and
because officials and officers get immense profits from
it, either as squeeze or as direot participants. As
indication of the laxity of this control im the eastward
movement during the past six month of sorap metal, copper
and medicines - all ∞mmodities desperately soarce in
Free China and strategio by any ∞nsideration.
Tung 011
The Japanese are probably getting from China all the
tung oil they need.
Sinoe the outbreak of the Sino-Japanese war tung oil
from Chekiang and other producing areas in East China has
been reaching Shanghai. In West China it was collected
by a sovermment monopoly organization for shipment to the
United States. After the alosing of the Burma Road, and
the development of dirfieulties in the tung oil gasoline
program, the monopoly discontinued purohases and permitted
the remumption of free private trade. Quantities are now
reaching the Japamese at Iohang and Hankow.
The same situation is true in regard to bristles,
except that the Japanese are probably unable to obtain
the best bristles from Sseohuan.
Wolfrom

=== Page 353 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
The Embassy has already infermed the Department of
Ohinm. The Chimese minimized these reports (see despatch
no. 117, September 4, 1941) but considerable quantities
of wolfram are still reaching the Japanese.
The wolfram is dug out of small holes by independent
operators who are required to sell it to the Chinese
govermment monopoly organization. The miners olaim that
the fixed monopoly prioe has not been raised in accordance
with other costs, and claim that they have suffered frem
unjust assay of their ore and inoorrect weighing. my 1940
the miners had found that they could get more for their
ore by taking it south to Swatow and Canton. This was in
effect robbing the monopoly, but government control of
the area of South Kiangsi concerned, (Ningtu and Juikin
hsien) has not been good sinoe these areas were Communist
strongholds, and there was undoubtedly corruption of
ofricials.
The Chinese Industrial Cooperatives investigated the
field and drew up a plan to organize the miners into
societies, endeavoring to secure for them better treat-
ment and compensation. This was turned down by the Chairman
of the Kiangsi Provinoial Government, General Hsiung Shih-
hui, (now in Washington) who was also head of the wolfram
purohasing organization.
Meanwhile the trade in wolfram with the Japanese
occupied area became so profitable that it was entered
into on a larger soale by merohants. It would have been
impossible for these to operate without official knowledge.
It is likely that officials concerned also profited.
Chiang Ching-kuo, the Generalissimo's son who was head
of an administrative distriet in Kiangsi, told Rewi Alley
that this was the situation but said that he was powerless.
Acoording to Alley, Wong Wen-hao also admitted the situa-
tion and said that "perhaps 50% was being lost". On my
reeent trip into Kansu, on whioh I traveled with a party
of National Resources Commission officials and rode a
trwas of wolfrom bound for Mussia, matter of fact reference
was made to this condition. My companions thought that
"between 1/3 and 1/4" of the wolfram mined was going
to the Japanese.
There have been recent indications that the Chinese
are having difrioulties in meeting wolfram commitments to
the United States and Russia.
Eeonomic Basis of Trade
There is no production in Free China of such semi-
luxury items of daily use as tooth-paste, shoe polish,
enamel ware, mirrors and so frth. There is only poor
qualrty and insurricient produetion of cigarettes and
cotton cloth. There is no ban on the importation of these
geods from the cecupied area and no rationing or attempt
to

=== Page 354 === (confidence 0.97)
liait comsumption in free Chins. In addition there
be emperted. This forms the base for an import trade of
very-large volume.
Free China has no manufaotures to expert so payment
for these imports ean be only by eurrency or raw materials.
The Japanese will acsept some Chinese sarreney (presummbly
because of diffioulty in emforcing its immediate abanden-
ment by the country people in the occupied areas) but only
at greatly depreciated valuation in terms of their own cur-
rencies, and oaly in certain denominations (thus wreaking
havoo with the ourrenoy in Honan and Shensi where $5 and
$lo bills have disappeared from ciroulation). It seems
obvlous that instead of cash, the Japanese prefer to
reeeive commodities of which they are in great need.
And they are able to attract these commodities by using
the Chimese currenoy, taken in ocoupied China and progres-
sively devaluated, to offer high prices. This seems like
a good move on the part of the Japanese: they obtain
strategio materials of which they are in great need with
the use of a ourreney which is of no use or value to them;
and, at the same time they deprive the Chinese, and their
allies, of these materials, they add to inflation in China
by pouring this currenoy back into the country.
Several things contribute to the Japanese success.
Some of these are not the fault of the Chinese government,
but some may be remedied.
The natural trade of Ningsia is with Suiyuan: oom-
munieati ons with Kansu are not good but might be improved.
Transportation of ∞tton from Honan to the rest of free
China might be made easier and something done at the same
time to develop loeal manufaoture: while raw cotton goes
east across the Yellow River, cotton aloth from Tientsin
and Shanghai comes west. The situation in regard to tung
oil is dirfioult unless further use for it in free China
is found.
In the cases of both wool and wolfram, an important
factor in the situation seems to be the operation of a
purchasing monopoly which restricts the earnings of the
actual producer. In both cases these monopolies seem to
opsrate on a large margin for overhead and profit: the
price to the producer is only a amall portion of the
price to the foreign purchaser. For instanee: I was told
that the Foo Shing Corporation is supplying second grade
woel to the Chinese Industrial Cooperatives at Lanchew for
Ch$7.50 a metrio pound (halr a kilogram), that the Chinghai
government receives $4.50 for this wool, but that the
Tibetan sheep owmer reseives $l.50. (The sorporation does
not preeess or clean the wool.) The Embassy has knewkedge
of dmilar sonditions in the operation of the bristle
monepoly.
John S. Service

Sir:
Referring to the Department's telegram No. 668 of
August 1l, 1942, transmitted on behalf of the Board of
Economio Warfare, I have the honor to enclose a tabula-
tion on Swedish exports of certain metal working machines,
ball and roller bearings, combustion and compression
motors, transmissions and gears to Japan or territories
occupied by Japan for the years 1935 te 1941, inelusive.
00T011914
There were no exports during 1942, according to the in-
FILEC
formation furnished by the Foreign Office.
The

=== Page 368 === (confidence 0.98)
- 8 -
The data ineluded in the tabulation enclosed, ealy
received from the Swedish Foreign Ofrice today, have
been compiled by the State Trade Commission.
In the Department's instruction information was
also requested regarding the names of Swedish shippers
as well as consignees of the equipment mentioned. The
Foreign Office, however, states that they are unable to
furnish this information. However, it was pointed out
that nearly all Swedish concerns manufacturing the items
mentioned have participated in these exports. The
biggest item, ball and roller bearings, were, of course,
shipped by the Swedish Ball Bearing Company (S.K.F.) in
Gothenburg.
The combustion and compression motors
mentioned in the tabulation were evidently not intended
for automobiles but were rather of the type used for
small oraft, which are manufactured by many companies
in Sweden.
The other large item, grinding machines, is com-
posed mainly of the so-called "centerless grinders".
Thewe are manufaotured by the Lidköpings Mekaniska Verk-
stads A/B., Lidkoping, a subsidiary or S.K.F., and also
by A/B. Malous Holmquist, Halmstad.
The Logation has learned that most of the Swedish
export business with Japan and adjacent countries has
been transaoted by two large Swedish agenoy houses,
Kjellbergs Suocessors, and Gadelius & Co., Stockholm,
whioh have branohes in Japan and several other oities
in the Far East, where they represent many important
Swedish industriel enterprises.
For the information of the Board of Economio Werfare
it is noted that the firm "Stal in Sweden" mentioned in

frigerating maohinery.

Dear Sir:
I have on board this vessel****
*motal junk which I loaded on my last call ***
On my next and final voyage there, (at least final for the
present, I hope) I'm taking a considerable additional quantity
of the same for the purpose of ballasting the ship on her voyage
from there to San Francisco, where I am going for dry-docking,
overhauling, etc.
Among this ballast I have a Japanese damaged 75-mm field
piece which was presented to me by those in charge of the captured
artillery dump on the beach at *** I wish to turn this gun to the
city of Sausalito, where I live, for permanent exibition in our
town park. This gun is government property, although I was told
to take it and as many more like it as I could haul away, as they
wanted to get rid of them. I consider this very flimsy title to
it, however, and wish to ask you if you will use your good auspices
with the War Department to obtain title to the damaged gun or
permission to turn it over to Sausalito. Either will be satisfactory.
非本非本
*********** I am anxious to have a favorable reply by the time I
arrive at Sem Frameisce, which I think will be about e
Otherwise, this field piece is liable to be dumped into a freight
car and sent East ***
If you address your reply to me, % Coastwise (Pacific Far East)
Line, Pier 35, San Francisco, it will be handed to me immediately
upon my arrival at that port.
Thanking you in advance for your help in this matter, I am,
Yours very truly
/S/ R. W. Look

=== Page 373 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF COMMERCIAL AFFAIRS
May 13, 1943.
MEMORANDUM OF MEETING HELD IN THE OFFICE
OF COLONEL SCOTT RITCHIE, ASSISTANT TO
GENERAL CAMPBELL, CHIEF OF ORDNANCE
Those present at the conference were as follows:
Colonel Ritchie, Ordnance
Colonel Mance, G-2, attached to Ordnance
as lialson officer
Lt. Col. Fonte of Col. Mance's office
Major Hobbeck, Oranance
Captain Kramer, Ordnance
Captain Davis, Ordnance
Mr. Peterson, Ordnance
Lieutenant McQuade, O.N.I.--Ordnance
Mr. Shirley Stephens of the Treasury Department,
representing Customs
Mr. Frederiok Exton, Department of State
The meeting was called in order to formulate a
policy regarding the importation of captured enemy
materiel whioh is arriving on the west coast particularly
(1) through ofricial shipments by the Army and Navy, (2)
through attempted importations by merchant captains and
crews of merchant vessels, (3) in soldiers' and sailors'
baggage, and (4) by paroel post shipments made by men of
the armed forces or by crews of merchant ships to families,
friends or commercials firms. These shipments are not
accompanied by import license issued by the Department.
The specific case which brought the matter to a
head, as explained by Mr. Stephens, was the notification
from the Honorable Clarence F. Lea, House of Repre-
sentatives, that a damaged Japanese 75 mm. field plece
was being imported by a merchant captain for display in
Sansalito, California. Congressman Lea had expressed
the desire that this importation be allowed and that the
town be given title to the gun.
Shipments under all four categories above, Colonel
Ritchie explained, were of the utmost importance to the
War

=== Page 374 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
War Department for the study of enemy weapons, proces-
ses, metals, etc. Up to the present those in category
(1) have been as a rule handled by the Army at the
ports of entry and transferred to Aberdeen where they
could be sorted and tested. The Navy has representatives
at Aberdeen who oan assist at these operations. These
shipments present no diffioulty, but it would be desirable
that a more uniform procedure be adopted and this will be
worked out by the G-2 officers attached to Army Ordnance
and the Naval Ordnance officers attached to the Office
of Naval Intelligence at all ports.
It was agreed that shipments coming under classi-
fication (2) should be seized by Customs and turned over
to Army and Navy officials at the port of entry. There
have been some irregularities at the ports due to mis-
interpretation of the regulations. Mr. Stephens will see
that all collectors of customs are adequately instruoted
for the future. Army and Navy officials are in agreement
that all materiel coming under this classifioation,
whether purchased, obtained by barter, or in any other
way where it is of sufficient size and importance to
warrant its being seized in the opinion of the Army or
Navy officers at ports of entry, should go to Aberdeen.
When G-2 and O.N.I. have completed their investigations,
they may be returned to the owner. This would probably
be at the end of the war. I agreed that these shipments
could not be interpreted as commercial transactions and
that the State Department was not interested in them un-
less they were consigned to an importer registered with
the Department. These cases would be rare.
Category (3) consists mostly of souvenirs brought
in by soldiers and sailors. It is important from a
morale point of view that as much of this materiel as
possible be returned to the soldier or sailor who is
bringing in souvenirs. Commanding officers are respon-
sible for the baggage of their men and further orders
will be given that the baggage should be carefully
scrutinized. If arms of no interest to G-2 and O.N.I.
are imported as souvenirs they may enter the country
without a license issued by the Department under para-
graph 20l.25 of the regulations.
Shipments of the type set forth under category (4)
would be confiscated by Customs and turned over to the
Army for examination. If found to be of no interest
they

=== Page 375 === (confidence 0.97)
-3
they will be sent on to :the addressee.
In the case of the damaged 75 mm. gun mentioned
above which falls under category (2), it will be
seized by Customs and transferred to the Army.  The
Army officers expressed the opinion that it would
probably be diffioult to make any claims to weapons of
this character until the close of the war, at which
time claims might be entertained.
74
DCA:FExton:EJF

=== Page 388 === (confidence 0.99)
1550-8. 14th Strost
Milwaukee, Wis. June 18th,1940
Hon. Senator Robt. La Follette,
Washington, D. C.
My Dear Senator:-
Attached I am sending you a newapaper clipping from the
Milwaukee Journal of May 18th, 1943.
You will note that the Journal calls the statement made
by Dr. Walter Judd, Republican Congressman from the Minneapolis
district "astounding", and certainly we oitizens think the
same.
My dear Senator, does Dr. Judd really mean to imply that
Uncle Sam is swapping supplies with our enemy----- or is Dr.
Judd talking thru his hat. If this statement which he made is
not true, then I think something should be done about keeping
men from "making false statements"; on the other hand, if it is
true what should the average loyal American citizen think.
You know my dear, Senator, that we here in Wisconsin are
buying bonds and doing everything we are asked to do, and trying
to be loyal in every way so that we win the war and the Peace,
but if we trading War supplies with our Enemy-----------then
will you please explain to me just how we can trade with
an enemy and still we are supposed to take thie War serious
and are told that we must do everything possible to defeat them;
in other words with one hand we try to beat them and with the
other we trade war materials and supplies with Japan.
As a loyal citizen-----I and many others here in good old
Milwaukee would like to know just what this article in the
Journal means.
Will you kindly.let me have a personal reply on thie, at
your convenience.
Either this article 1s true or false-----and if it is not
true then men like Dr. Judd should be made, to prove what they
are handing out to the people, who I am sure mean to be loyal
but sometimes are confused by just such statements as this one.
Thanking you for your kindnesses in the past, with best
wishes, I remain,
Very truly yours,
RAY HENNINGFELD
1550-8. 14th Street
Milwaukee, Wis.
P.S.---Will you please return this newspaper article to me
when you have finished using it in whatever way you
may want to use it.

=== Page 397 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure no. 1 to despatch no. 1503 dated August 24,
1943 from American Embassy, Chungking, China.
JAPANESE COLLECTING METALLIC ARTICLES IN OCCUPIED AREAS
Separate Organizations Set Up in Shanghai
to Comb Regions Under Occupation for Metals
Tungki, Anhwei, (Central ews Agency).- Indicative of
acute metal shortage in their war industries, Jananese
authorities in occupied China, especially in the coastal
regions, have set up machineries for "collecting metallic
articles" from the people.
Reports from Shanghai stated that recently three
separate organizations had been set up by the occupational
authorities, each cherged with the duty of combing the
entire regions under their "jurisdiction" for metals of all
descrintions. Particular emnhaas has been placed upon
iron and steel and brass, which ere much needed in Jananese
war industries.
The three organizations, the reports said, are the
"Committee in Charge of Requisitioning Brasswares", "Com-
mittee in Charge of Requisitioning Essential Materials" and
the "Committee in Charge of Requisitioning 'Waste' Articles".
In carrying out their functions, the committees have not
spared such sacred objects as idols in a temple and bells in
a public place of worshin.
Investigators have been sent out by the committees, the
reports said, to check un all structures built of materials
which can be used in war manufactories. Gates built of cast
iron have been requisitioned in many cities, it was leamed.
The same investigators were said to have invaded even
private homes and removed therefrom such articles as candle-
sticks and metallic lamns. In fact, the investigators have
left no stone unturned in pursuins their duties, the reports
added.
Earlier reports stated that Jananese had systema-
tically removed statues which for many years had been land-
marks in Shanghai. It was believed that the order for
taking down the statues had been issued by one of the three
newly set up committees.
Source: National Herald, Chungking, August 18, 1943.

19
894.24/1902
Gentlemen:
In one of your recent programs you stated you would be
glad to answer questions regarding war matters and I
would appreciate very much your reply to our question.
I operate a small manufacturing plant and in the interest
894.24
of this I travel all over the United States. For several
years prior to Pearl Harbor, when in such towns as New
Orleans, Mobile, Houston, Galveston, Corpus Christi, Port
Arthur, and etc. I would notice Jap ships being loaded
with scrap iron, metals, and etc.
In 1939 the Bottlers Convention was held in San Francisco
and we were there about ten days. During this time we
noticed a number of Jap ships there being loaded with
scrap iron and other metals.
It was generally discussed at that time that eventually
this would be returned to us from the "wrong end of tanks
and guns." Some commentators and periodicals were open-
ingly prediciting the same thing.
The Bottlers Convention was held in Philadelphia in 194l--
PS/HWL
just about the time the Japs were slowly moving down into
the South Pacific, and en route home, my two sons, my
secretary, and myself were discussing the Japanese situation
and we were all of the opinion that it was "war with Japan".
What I can't understand is, if ordinary people could see
this, why couldn't someone have put a stop to the flow of
this material to Japan? What could our boys, who are now
sacrificing their lifes, have done to prevent the flow of
that material to Japan?
SEP 2
Yours very truly,
S.b. Eoans

Sir:
894.24/1905
1938 apparenth,
During 1928, The Dow Chemical Company accepted in the
350 Fifth Avenue, New York City, totaling 1,525,O27 pounds of
pure, ingot magnesium and in accordance with instructions, duly
shipped the orders to them f.a.s. New York. We did not inquire
whether or not the magnesium was for the account of the Japanese
Government or the use to which it was to be put, since we were
not asked for technological service.
894.24
On Monday, March 6, 1944, while testifying before the
Senate Committee investigating the National Defense Program, I
was asked by counsel for the Committee if we had obtained
clearance for the shipment from the Department of State and
also why we had not investigated the use to which the magnesium
was to be put. The inference or innuendo was that we had in
some fashion neglected our duty as citizens.
I am writing to discover if you will be good enough to
inform me whether or not this Company failed to perform any duty
in the premises which it should properly have performed. Seven
shipments in all were made during 1938.
I am
APR 12 1944

=== Page 420 === (confidence 0.98)
re No. 1 to despatch No. 2474, April 22, 1944
Subject of Japanese Armament Production in 1942.
CRET
(Translation)
INCREASED PRODUCTION OF ARMAMENTS IN JAPAN
C.
In the week prior to Matsuoka's arrival in Germany
(in the summer of 194l) the Japanese military mission to
Germany, headed by General Yamashita, signed a German-
greFs
Japanese technical assistance pact providing for German
assistance for technical improvement of Japanese arma-
ments production. In order to implement the pact the Ger-
man Government comnissioned 16 experts selected from the
Spandau, Danzig, Krupp, Rhein, Suhl and Moser factories
to be sent to Japan as a mission headed by Brigadier
General Oppenheim. (Oppenheim was the liaison officer be-
tween the German Government and the Krupp Works). The
mission was divided into five sections to specialize on
the following: (1) automatic weapons; (2) artillery;
(3) tanks; (4) explosives including mines and al1 types
of bombs, and (5) technical improvements and planning.
After their arrival in Jepan the members of the
mission spent 40 days making an investigation of the ar-
maments industries. They thereupon drew up a detailed
report, including recommendations, which was submitted to
the Japanese Government on January 26, 1942. In the plan
much emphasis was laid upon rationalization of war pro-
duction. Hence, the following basic principles were re-
commended by the mission for immediate adoption: (1) uni-
fication of control over all armaments industries; (2)
strengthening of liaison between the Government and pri-
vate plants engaged in war production; (3) reorganization
of armaments plants with a view to increasing output; (4)
immediate construction of underground facilities to house
key plants as a precaution against air raids, and (5) es-
tablishment of new plants and expansion of existing plants.
Detailed recommendations on the fo regoing were sub-
mitted by the mission to the National Mobilization Council.
These recommendations were carefully studied by the Coun-
cil which, after heated debate, approved all except the
first regarding unification of control over all armaments
industries. Adoption of this recommendation was strongly
advocated by the Army but opposed by the Navy, Commerce
and Industry and Finance ministries. Therefore this re-
commendation was shelved for future discussion. The other
recommendations were accepted and put into force on March
1, 1942. The attached tables show the output of armaments
for the 10-month period beginning with March 1942 (com-
pared with the output during the first two months of 1942).
General Remarks on Tables Showing Output of Government-
Controlled and Privately-Owned Armaments Plants.
1. Japan had already commenced to mobilize in 1940.
In 194l armaments production was further increased. Even
with German technical assistance the resulting increase in
output was only double. The reason is that the foundations
of the Japanese armaments industries have always been very
poor.

=== Page 421 === (confidence 0.99)
.一 2 -.
or. However, according to a report of the Cabinet's
lanning Board, the new plan resulted in a saving of labor
of 8.7 percent. A general increase of production was ex-
pected after February 1943 owing to completion of new
equipment.
2. The tables were taken from certain files of the
Planning Board and the authenticity thereof is unquestioned.
3. The number of employees represents those directly
employed. The comparison of men and women workers is based
on figures for 1942.
4. Regarding poison gases, it is understood that they
are produced by the Mitsui, Mitsubishi, Tokyo and Osaka
factories but no details are available.
5. The number of workers shown in the tables is as of
the end of December 1942.
6. The production plan for 1943 is understood to have
been drafted in 1942 but cannot yet be obtained.
7. A munitions plant was established in Formosa in
August l942 for production of bullets, artillery shells,
bombs, et detere. It was expected to be completed by the
end of 1943. In Niigata .ork has been started on construc-
tion of an underground factory to house the machinery de-
partment of an armaments plant.
Other Remarks.
1. Tojo, after the close of the 8lst session of the
Diet, received the German, Italian and Spanish ambassadors
and spoke to them of the Japanese armaments industries as
follows: "With the exception of airplanes and ships, Japan
finds its armaments production highly satisfactory".
2. The Japanese Ambassador to Germany is said to have
expressed the opinion that Japan finds herself satisfac-
torily equipped in everything for the decisive war but that
she is still in need of technical assistance and also help
for development of secret weapons.
3. Colonel Malaky, the German Military Attache at
Tokyo, stated that the rapid increase in Japanese produc-
tion of armaments greatly exceeded all expectations of
Europeans and that it was due largely to the imitative
ability and hard work of Japanese labor. All this, Colonel
Malaky stated, "deserves our highest admiration".  (This
was reported in the Deutschland in April 1943).

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Washington, D. C.
Dear Dr. Hornbeck:
Referring to our telephone conversation you will
be very pleased to know that after a further talk with Mr.
Wilkinson it was agreed that we would send the telegram to
894.24/4-1641
Yokohama as originally drafted, which was telephoned to
you yesterday by Mr. Walden and later quoted in his letter
to you which was sent earlier today.
Mr. Wilkinson further stated that he would
cable back to London the gist of the views which I pessed
on to him in explanation of why the message was sent as
originally drafted. They may pursue this matter further
at a later date but I am very pleased that for the moment they
have entirely accepted your views.
Sincerely yours,
